Spatially-Periodic Solutions for Evolution Anisotropic Variable-Coefficient Navier-Stokes Equations: II. Serrin-Type Solutions
Sergey E. Mikhailov

Abstract
We consider evolution (non-stationary) space-periodic solutions to the n n -dimensional non-linear Navier-Stokes equations of anisotropic fluids with the viscosity coefficient tensor variable in space and time and satisfying the relaxed ellipticity condition. Employing the Galerkin algorithm, we prove the existence of Serrin-type solutions, that is, weak solutions with the velocity in the periodic space L 2 ​ ( 0 , T , 𝐇 ˙ # ​ σ n / 2 ) L_{2}(0,T;\dot{\mathbf{H}}^{n/2}_{\#\sigma}) , n ≥ 2 n\geq 2 . The solution uniqueness and regularity results are also discussed. Keywords . Partial differential equations; Evolution Navier-Stokes equations; Anisotropic Navier-Stokes; Spatially periodic solutions; Variable coefficients; Relaxed ellipticity condition; Serrin-type solutions. MSC classes : 35A1, 35B10, 35K45, 35Q30, 76D05



1
Introduction

Analysis of Stokes and Navier-Stokes equations is an established and active field of research in applied mathematical analysis; see, e.g.,
[
15
,
18
,
7
,
16
,
17
,
10
,
27
,
30
,
33
,
35
,
36
]
and many other publications.
These works were mainly devoted to the flows of isotropic fluids with constant viscosity coefficient, and some of the employed methods were heavily based on these properties.

On the other hand, in many cases the fluid viscosity can vary in time and spatial coordinates, e.g., due to variable ambient temperature.
Moreover, from the point of view of rational mechanics of continuum, fluids can be anisotropic and this feature is indeed observed in liquid crystals, electromagnetic fluids, etc., see, e.g.,
[
8
]
and references therein.
In
[
11
,
12
,
13
,
14
,
22
,
23
]
the classical Navier-Stokes analysis has been extended to the transmission and boundary-value problems for
stationary
Stokes and Navier-Stokes equations of anisotropic fluids, particularly with relaxed ellipticity condition on the viscosity tensor.

In Part I,
[
24
]
, we considered
evolution (non-stationary)
spatially-periodic solutions in
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
,
n\geq 2
, to the Navier-Stokes equations of an anisotropic fluid with the viscosity coefficient tensor variable in spatial coordinates and time and satisfying the relaxed ellipticity condition.
We implemented the Galerkin algorithm but unlike the traditional approach, for example in
[
35
,
36
]
, where the Galerkin basis consisted of the eigenfunctions of the corresponding isotropic constant-coefficient Stokes operator, we employed the basis constituted by the eigenfunctions of the periodic Bessel-potential operator having an advantage that it is universal, i.e., independent of the analysed anisotropic variable-coefficient Navier-Stokes operator.
To analyse the solution in higher dimensions, the definition of the weak solution was generalised to some extent.
Then the periodic weak solution existence was considered in the spaces of Banach-valued functions mapping a finite time interval to periodic Sobolev (Bessel-potential) spaces on
n
-dimensional flat torus,
L_{\infty}(0,T;\dot{\mathbf{H}}_{\#\sigma}^{0})\cap L_{2}(0,T;\dot{\mathbf{H}}_{\#\sigma}^{1})
.
The periodic setting is interesting on its own, modelling fluid flow in periodic composite structures, and is also a common element of homogenisation theories for inhomogeneous fluids and in the Large Eddy Simulation.

In this paper, Part II, we prove the existence, uniqueness and regularity of the weak solutions that belongs to the space
L_{2}(0,T;\dot{\mathbf{H}}^{n/2}_{\#\sigma})
(we call them Serrin-type solutions).
It is well known that the regularity results available at the moment for evolution Navier-Stokes equations are rather different for dimensions
n=2
and
n=3
, even for isotropic constant-viscosity fluids.
The weak solution global regularity under arbitrarily large smooth input data for
n=2
is proved and can be found, e.g., in
[
15
,
18
,
7
,
16
,
10
,
27
,
30
,
33
,
35
,
36
]
. However for
n=3
it is still an open question and constitutes one of the Clay Institute famous Millennium problems.
Our motivation for considering arbitrary
n\geq 2
is particularly to place the cases
n=2
and
n=3
in a more general set and to see which of them is an exception.

The paper material is presented as follows. In Section
1.1
we provide essentials on anisotropic Stokes and Navier-Stokes equations.
Section
1.2
gives an introduction to the periodic Sobolev (Bessel-potential) functions spaces in spatial coordinates on
n
-dimensional flat torus and to the corresponding Banach-valued functions mapping a finite time interval to these periodic Sobolev spaces.
In Section
2
we describe the existence results for evolution spatially-periodic anisotropic Navier-Stokes problem available from Part I,
[
24
]
.
Sections
3
,
4
and
5
contain the main results of the paper.
In Section
3
we define the Serrin-type solutions and prove the energy equality for them and also their uniqueness, for the
n
-dimensional periodic setting,
n\geq 2
. We also remark on their relations with the strong solutions and show that for
n=2
, any weak solution is a Serrin-type solution.
In Section
4
we analyse the Serrin-type solution existence and regularity for constant anisotropic viscosity coefficients,
while in Section
5
we generalise these results to variable anisotropic viscosity coefficients.
In Section
6
we collect some technical results used in the main text of the paper, several of which might be new.

1.1
Anisotropic Stokes and Navier-Stokes PDE systems

Let
n\geq 2
be an integer,
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
denote the space coordinate vector, and
t\in{\mathbb{R}}
be time.
Let

\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{L}}
denote the second-order differential operator represented in the component-wise divergence form as

\displaystyle(\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{L}}{\mathbf{u}})_{k}:=\partial_{\alpha}\big(a_{kj}^{\alpha\beta}E_{j\beta}({\mathbf{u}})\big),\ \ k=1,\ldots,n,

(1.1)

where
{\mathbf{u}}\!=\!(u_{1},\ldots,u_{n})^{\top}
,

\displaystyle E_{j\beta}({\mathbf{u}})\!:=\!\frac{1}{2}(\partial_{j}u_{\beta}+\partial_{\beta}u_{j})

(1.2)

are the entries of the symmetric part,
{\mathbb{E}}({\mathbf{u}})
, of the gradient,
\nabla{\mathbf{u}}
, in space coordinates,
and
a_{kj}^{\alpha\beta}(\mathbf{x},t)
are variable components of the tensor viscosity coefficient, cf.
[
8
]
,

{\mathbb{A}}(\mathbf{x},t)=\!\left\{{a_{kj}^{\alpha\beta}}(\mathbf{x},t)\right\}_{1\leq i,j,\alpha,\beta\leq n}
, depending on the space coordinate vector
\mathbf{x}
and time
t
.
We also denoted
\partial_{j}=\dfrac{\partial}{\partial x_{j}}
,
\partial_{t}=\dfrac{\partial}{\partial t}
.
Here and further on, the Einstein convention on summation in repeated indices from
1
to
n
is used unless stated otherwise.

The following symmetry conditions are assumed (see
[
26
, (3.1),(3.3)]
),

\displaystyle a_{kj}^{\alpha\beta}(\mathbf{x},t)=a_{\alpha j}^{k\beta}(\mathbf{x},t)=a_{k\beta}^{\alpha j}(\mathbf{x},t).

(1.3)

In addition, we require that tensor
{\mathbb{A}}
satisfies the relaxed ellipticity condition in terms of all
symmetric
matrices in
{\mathbb{R}}^{n\times n}
with
zero matrix trace
, see
[
12
]
,
[
13
]
. Thus, we assume that there exists a constant
C_{\mathbb{A}}>0
such that,

\displaystyle C_{\mathbb{A}}a_{kj}^{\alpha\beta}(\mathbf{x},t)\zeta_{k\alpha}\zeta_{j\beta}\geq|\boldsymbol{\zeta}|^{2}\,,\ \ \mbox{for a.e. }\mathbf{x},t,

(1.4)

\displaystyle\forall\ \boldsymbol{\zeta}=\{\zeta_{k\alpha}\}_{k,\alpha=1,\ldots,n}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n\times n}\mbox{ such that }\,\boldsymbol{\zeta}=\boldsymbol{\zeta}^{\top}\mbox{ and }\sum_{k=1}^{n}\zeta_{kk}=0,

where
|\boldsymbol{\zeta}|=|\boldsymbol{\zeta}|_{F}:=(\zeta_{k\alpha}\zeta_{k\alpha})^{1/2}
is the Frobenius matrix norm and the superscript
⊤
denotes the transpose of a matrix.
Note that in the more common, strong ellipticity condition (called S-ellipticity condition in
[
25
, Definition 4.1]
), inequality (
1.4
) should be satisfied for all matrices (not only symmetric with zero trace), which makes it much more restrictive (cf. also E-class in
[
26
, Section 3.1]
, where condition (
1.4
) is assumed for all symmetric matrices).

We assume that
a_{ij}^{\alpha\beta}\in L_{\infty}({\mathbb{R}}^{n}\times[0,T])
, where
[0,T]
is some finite time interval, and the tensor
{\mathbb{A}}
is endowed with the norm

\displaystyle\|{\mathbb{A}}\|:=\|{\mathbb{A}}\|_{L_{\infty}({\mathbb{R}}^{n}\times[0,T]),F}:=\left|\left\{\|a_{ij}^{\alpha\beta}\|_{L_{\infty}({\mathbb{T}}\times[0,T])}\right\}_{\alpha,\beta,i,j=1}^{n}\right|_{F}<\infty,

(1.5)

where
\left|\left\{b_{ij}^{\alpha\beta}\right\}_{\alpha,\beta,i,j=1}^{n}\right|_{F}:=\left(b_{ij}^{\alpha\beta}b_{ij}^{\alpha\beta}\right)^{1/2}
is the Frobenius norm of a 4-th order tensor.

Symmetry conditions (
1.3
) lead to the following equivalent form of the operator
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{L}}

(\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{L}}{\mathbf{u}})_{k}=\partial_{\alpha}\big(a_{kj}^{\alpha\beta}\partial_{\beta}u_{j}\big),\ \ k=1,\ldots,n.

(1.6)

Let
{\mathbf{u}}(\mathbf{x},t)
be an unknown vector velocity field,
p(\mathbf{x},t)
be an unknown (scalar) pressure field, and
{\mathbf{f}}(\mathbf{x},t)
be a given vector field

{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
, where
t\in{\mathbb{R}}
is the time variable.
The nonlinear system

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\mathbf{u}-\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{L}}{\mathbf{u}}+\nabla p+({\mathbf{u}}\cdot\nabla){\mathbf{u}}={\mathbf{f}}\,,\ \ {\rm{div}}\,{\mathbf{u}}=0

is the
evolution anisotropic incompressible Navier-Stokes system
, where we use the notation
({\mathbf{u}}\cdot\nabla):=u_{j}\partial_{j}
.

1.2
Periodic function spaces

Let us introduce some function spaces on torus, i.e., periodic function spaces (see, e.g.,

[
1
, p.26]
,
[
2
]
,
[
20
]
,
[
29
, Chapter 3]
,
[
27
, Section 1.7.1]
,

[
35
, Chapter 2]

for more details).

Let
n\geq 1
be an integer and
{\mathbb{T}}
be the
n
-dimensional flat torus that can be parametrized as the semi-open cube
{\mathbb{T}}={\mathbb{T}}^{n}=[0,1)^{n}\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
, cf.
[
37
, p. 312]
.
In what follows,
{\mathcal{D}}({\mathbb{T}})=\mathcal{C}^{\infty}({\mathbb{T}})
denotes the (test) space of infinitely smooth real or complex functions on the torus.
As usual,
{\mathbb{N}}
denotes the set of natural numbers,
{\mathbb{N}}_{0}
the set of natural numbers augmented by 0, and
\mathbb{Z}
the set of integers.

Let
\boldsymbol{\xi}\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}
denote the
n
-dimensional vector with integer components.
We will further need also the set

\dot{\mathbb{Z}}^{n}:=\mathbb{Z}^{n}\setminus\{\mathbf{0}\}.

Extending the torus parametrisation to
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
, it is often useful to identify
{\mathbb{T}}
with the quotient space
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\setminus\mathbb{Z}^{n}
.
Then the space of functions
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}({\mathbb{T}})
on the torus can be identified with the space of
{\mathbb{T}}
-periodic (1-periodic) functions

\mathcal{C}^{\infty}_{\#}=\mathcal{C}^{\infty}_{\#}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})
that consists of functions
\phi\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})
such that

\displaystyle\phi(\mathbf{x}+\boldsymbol{\xi})=\phi(\mathbf{x})\quad\forall\,\boldsymbol{\xi}\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}.

(1.7)

Similarly, the Lebesgue space on the torus
L_{p}({\mathbb{T}})
,
1\leq p\leq\infty
, can be identified with the periodic Lebesgue space
L_{p\#}=L_{p\#}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})
that consists of functions
\phi\in L_{p,\rm loc}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})
, which satisfy the periodicity condition (
1.7
) for a.e.
\mathbf{x}
.

The space dual to
\mathcal{D}({\mathbb{T}})
, i.e., the space of linear bounded functionals on
\mathcal{D}({\mathbb{T}})
, called the space of torus distributions, is denoted by
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}({\mathbb{T}})
and can be identified with the space of periodic distributions
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{\#}
acting on
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}_{\#}
.

The toroidal/periodic Fourier transform
mapping a function
g\in\mathcal{C}_{\#}^{\infty}
to a set of its Fourier coefficients
\hat{g}
is defined as (see, e.g.,
[
29
, Definition 3.1.8]
)

\displaystyle\hat{g}(\boldsymbol{\xi})=[{\cal F}_{{\mathbb{T}}}g](\boldsymbol{\xi}):=\int_{{\mathbb{T}}}e^{-2\pi i\mathbf{x}\cdot\boldsymbol{\xi}}g(\mathbf{x})d\mathbf{x},\quad\boldsymbol{\xi}\in\mathbb{Z}^{n},

and can be generalised to the Fourier transform acting on a distribution
g\in\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{\#}
.

For any
\boldsymbol{\xi}\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}
, let
|\boldsymbol{\xi}|:=(\sum_{j=1}^{n}\xi_{j}^{2})^{1/2}
be the Euclidean norm in
\mathbb{Z}^{n}
and let us denote

\displaystyle\varrho(\boldsymbol{\xi}):=2\pi(1+|\boldsymbol{\xi}|^{2})^{1/2}.

(1.8)

Evidently,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\varrho(\boldsymbol{\xi})^{2}\leq|2\pi\boldsymbol{\xi}|^{2}\leq\varrho(\boldsymbol{\xi})^{2}\quad\forall\,\boldsymbol{\xi}\in\dot{\mathbb{Z}}^{n}.

(1.9)

Similar to
[
29
, Definition 3.2.2]
, for
s\in{\mathbb{R}}
we define the
periodic/toroidal Sobolev (Bessel-potential) spaces

H_{\#}^{s}:=H_{\#}^{s}({\mathbb{R}}^{n}):=H^{s}({\mathbb{T}})
that consist of the torus distributions
g\in\mathcal{D}^{\prime}({\mathbb{T}})
, for which the norm

\displaystyle\|g\|_{H_{\#}^{s}}:=\|\varrho^{s}\widehat{g}\|_{\boldsymbol{\ell}_{2}(\mathbb{Z}^{n})}:=\left(\sum_{\boldsymbol{\xi}\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}}\varrho(\boldsymbol{\xi})^{2s}|\widehat{g}(\boldsymbol{\xi})|^{2}\right)^{1/2}

(1.10)

is finite, i.e., the series in (
1.10
) converges.
Here
\|\cdot\|_{\boldsymbol{\ell}_{2}(\mathbb{Z}^{n})}
is the standard norm in the space of square summable sequences with indices in
\mathbb{Z}^{n}
.
By
[
29
, Proposition 3.2.6]
,
H_{\#}^{s}
is the Hilbert space with
the inner (scalar) product in
H_{\#}^{s}
defined as

\displaystyle(g,f)_{H_{\#}^{s}}:=\sum_{\boldsymbol{\xi}\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}}\varrho(\boldsymbol{\xi})^{2s}\hat{g}(\boldsymbol{\xi})\overline{\hat{f}(\boldsymbol{\xi})},\quad\forall\,g,f\in H_{\#}^{s},\ s\in{\mathbb{R}},

(1.11)

where the bar denotes complex conjugate.
Evidently,
H_{\#}^{0}=L_{2\#}
.

The dual product between
g\in H_{\#}^{s}
and
f\in H_{\#}^{-s}
,
s\in{\mathbb{R}}
, is defined (cf.
[
29
, Definition 3.2.8]
) as

\displaystyle\langle g,f\rangle_{{\mathbb{T}}}:=\sum_{\boldsymbol{\xi}\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}}\hat{g}(\boldsymbol{\xi})\hat{f}(-\boldsymbol{\xi}).

(1.12)

If
s=0
, i.e.,
g,f\in L_{2\#}
, then (
1.11
) and (
1.12
) reduce to

\langle g,f\rangle_{{\mathbb{T}}}=\int_{{\mathbb{T}}}g(\mathbf{x})f(\mathbf{x})d\mathbf{x}=(g,\bar{f})_{L_{2\#}}.

For real function
g,f\in L_{2\#}
we, of course, have
\langle g,f\rangle_{{\mathbb{T}}}=(g,f)_{L_{2\#}}
.

For any
s\in{\mathbb{R}}
, the space
H_{\#}^{-s}
is Banach adjoint (dual) to
H_{\#}^{s}
, i.e.,
H_{\#}^{-s}=(H_{\#}^{s})^{*}
.
Similar to, e.g.,
[
21
, p.76]
one can show that

\displaystyle\|g\|_{H_{\#}^{s}}=\sup_{f\in H_{\#}^{-s},f\neq 0}\frac{|\langle g,f\rangle_{{\mathbb{T}}}|}{\|f\|_{H_{\#}^{-s}}}.

For
g\in H_{\#}^{s}
,
s\in{\mathbb{R}}
, and
m\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}
, let us consider the partial sums

g_{m}(\mathbf{x})=\sum_{\boldsymbol{\xi}\in\mathbb{Z}^{n},|\boldsymbol{\xi}|\leq m}\hat{g}(\boldsymbol{\xi})e^{2\pi i\mathbf{x}\cdot\boldsymbol{\xi}}.

Evidently,
g_{m}\in\mathcal{C}_{\#}^{\infty}
,
\hat{g}_{m}(\boldsymbol{\xi})=\hat{g}(\boldsymbol{\xi})
if
|\boldsymbol{\xi}|\leq m
and
\hat{g}_{m}(\boldsymbol{\xi})=0
if
|\boldsymbol{\xi}|>m
.
This implies that
\|g-g_{m}\|_{H_{\#}^{s}}\to 0
as
m\to\infty
and hence we can write

\displaystyle g(\mathbf{x})=\sum_{\boldsymbol{\xi}\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}}\hat{g}(\boldsymbol{\xi})e^{2\pi i\mathbf{x}\cdot\boldsymbol{\xi}},

(1.13)

where the Fourier series converges in the sense of norm (
1.10
).
Moreover, since
g
is an arbitrary distribution from
H_{\#}^{s}
, this also implies that the space
{\mathcal{C}}^{\infty}_{\#}
is dense in
H_{\#}^{s}
for any
s\in{\mathbb{R}}
(cf.
[
29
, Exercise 3.2.9]
).

There holds the compact embedding
H_{\#}^{t}\hookrightarrow H_{\#}^{s}
if
t>s
, embeddings
H_{\#}^{s}\subset\mathcal{C}_{\#}^{m}
if
m\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}
,
s>m+\frac{n}{2}
, and moreover,
\bigcap_{s\in{\mathbb{R}}}H_{\#}^{s}={\mathcal{C}}^{\infty}_{\#}
(cf.
[
29
, Exercises 3.2.10, 3.2.10, and Corollary 3.2.11]
).

Note that for each
s
, the periodic norm (
1.10
) is equivalent to the periodic norm that we used in
[
22
,
23
]
, which is obtained from (
1.10
) by replacing there
\varrho(\boldsymbol{\xi})=2\pi(1+|\boldsymbol{\xi}|^{2})^{1/2}
with
\rho(\boldsymbol{\xi})=(1+|\boldsymbol{\xi}|^{2})^{1/2}
.
We employ here the norm (
1.10
) to simplify some norm estimates further in the paper.
Note also that the periodic norms on
H_{\#}^{s}
are equivalent to the corresponding standard (non-periodic) Bessel potential norms on
{\mathbb{T}}
as an
n-
cubic domain, see, e.g.,
[
2
, Section 13.8.1]
.

Let

\displaystyle\left(\Lambda_{\#}^{r}\,{g}\right)(\mathbf{x})

\displaystyle:=\sum_{\boldsymbol{\xi}\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}}\varrho(\boldsymbol{\xi})^{r}\hat{g}(\boldsymbol{\xi})e^{2\pi i\mathbf{x}\cdot\boldsymbol{\xi}}\quad\forall\,g\in H_{\#}^{s}.

(1.14)

denote the periodic Bessel-potential operator of the order
r\in{\mathbb{R}}
.
For any
s\in{\mathbb{R}}
, the operator

\displaystyle\Lambda_{\#}^{r}

\displaystyle:{H}_{\#}^{s}\to{H}_{\#}^{s-r},

(1.15)

is continuous, see, e.g.,
[
2
, Section 13.8.1]
.

By (
1.10
),

\|g\|^{2}_{H_{\#}^{s}}=|\widehat{g}(\mathbf{0})|^{2}+|g|^{2}_{H_{\#}^{s}},

where

\displaystyle|g|_{H_{\#}^{s}}:=\|\varrho^{s}\widehat{g}\|_{\boldsymbol{\ell}_{2}(\dot{\mathbb{Z}}^{n})}:=\left(\sum_{\boldsymbol{\xi}\in\dot{\mathbb{Z}}^{n}}\varrho(\boldsymbol{\xi})^{2s}|\widehat{g}(\boldsymbol{\xi})|^{2}\right)^{1/2}

is the seminorm in
H_{\#}^{s}
.

For any
s\in{\mathbb{R}}
, let us also introduce the space

\displaystyle\dot{H}_{\#}^{s}:=\{g\in H_{\#}^{s}:\langle g,1\rangle_{{\mathbb{T}}}=0\}.

The definition implies that if
g\in\dot{H}_{\#}^{s}
, then
\widehat{g}(\mathbf{0})=0
and

\displaystyle\|g\|_{\dot{H}_{\#}^{s}}=\|g\|_{H_{\#}^{s}}=|g|_{H_{\#}^{s}}=\|\varrho^{s}\widehat{g}\|_{\boldsymbol{\ell}_{2}(\dot{\mathbb{Z}}^{n})}\ .

(1.16)

The space
\dot{H}_{\#}^{s}
is the Hilbert space with inner product inherited from (
1.11
), that is,

\displaystyle(g_{1},g_{2})_{\dot{H}_{\#}^{s}}:=\sum_{\boldsymbol{\xi}\in\dot{\mathbb{Z}}^{n}}\varrho(\boldsymbol{\xi})^{2s}\hat{g}_{1}(\boldsymbol{\xi})\overline{\hat{g}_{2}(\boldsymbol{\xi})},\quad\forall\,g_{1},g_{2}\in\dot{H}_{\#}^{s},\ s\in{\mathbb{R}}.

(1.17)

The dual product between
g_{1}\in\dot{H}_{\#}^{s}
and
f_{2}\in(\dot{H}_{\#}^{s})^{*}
,
s\in{\mathbb{R}}
, is represented as

\displaystyle\langle g_{1},f_{2}\rangle_{{\mathbb{T}}}:=\sum_{\boldsymbol{\xi}\in\dot{\mathbb{Z}}^{n}}\hat{g}_{1}(\boldsymbol{\xi})\hat{f}_{2}(-\boldsymbol{\xi}).

If
\hat{g}(\mathbf{0})=0
then (
1.14
) implies that
\widehat{\Lambda_{\#}^{r}\,g}(\mathbf{0})=0
, and
thus the operator

\displaystyle\Lambda_{\#}^{r}:\dot{H}_{\#}^{s}\to\dot{H}_{\#}^{s-r}

(1.18)

is continuous as well.
Due to the Riesz representation theorem,

(\dot{H}_{\#}^{s})^{*}=\dot{H}_{\#}^{-s}
, as shown in
[
24
, Section 2]
.

Denoting

\dot{\mathcal{C}}^{\infty}_{\#}:=\{g\in{\mathcal{C}}^{\infty}_{\#}:\langle g,1\rangle_{{\mathbb{T}}}=0\}
,
then
\bigcap_{s\in{\mathbb{R}}}\dot{H}_{\#}^{s}=\dot{\mathcal{C}}^{\infty}_{\#}
.
The corresponding spaces of
n
-component vector functions/distributions are denoted as
\mathbf{L}_{q\#}:=(L_{q\#})^{n}
,
\mathbf{H}_{\#}^{s}:=(H_{\#}^{s})^{n}
, etc.

Note that the norm
\|\nabla(\cdot)\|_{{\mathbf{H}}_{\#}^{s-1}}

is an equivalent norm in
\dot{H}_{\#}^{s}
.
Indeed, by (
1.13
)

\displaystyle\nabla g(\mathbf{x})=2\pi i\sum_{\boldsymbol{\xi}\in\dot{\mathbb{Z}}^{n}}\boldsymbol{\xi}e^{2\pi i\mathbf{x}\cdot\boldsymbol{\xi}}\hat{g}(\boldsymbol{\xi}),\quad\widehat{\nabla g}(\boldsymbol{\xi})=2\pi i\boldsymbol{\xi}\hat{g}(\boldsymbol{\xi})\quad\forall\,g\in\dot{H}_{\#}^{s},

and
then (
1.9
) and (
1.16
) imply

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}|g|^{2}_{H_{\#}^{s}}

\displaystyle\leq\|\nabla g\|^{2}_{{\mathbf{H}}_{\#}^{s-1}}\leq|g|^{2}_{H_{\#}^{s}}\quad\forall\,g\in H_{\#}^{s},

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\|g\|^{2}_{H_{\#}^{s}}=\frac{1}{2}\|g\|^{2}_{\dot{H}_{\#}^{s}}=\frac{1}{2}|g|^{2}_{H_{\#}^{s}}

\displaystyle\leq\|\nabla g\|^{2}_{{\mathbf{H}}_{\#}^{s-1}}\leq|g|^{2}_{H_{\#}^{s}}=\|g\|^{2}_{\dot{H}_{\#}^{s}}=\|g\|^{2}_{H_{\#}^{s}}\quad\forall\,g\in\dot{H}_{\#}^{s}.

(1.19)

The vector counterpart of (
1.19
) takes form

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\|\mathbf{v}\|^{2}_{{\mathbf{H}}_{\#}^{s}}=\frac{1}{2}\|\mathbf{v}\|^{2}_{\dot{\mathbf{H}}_{\#}^{s}}\leq\|\nabla\mathbf{v}\|^{2}_{(H_{\#}^{s-1})^{n\times n}}\leq\|\mathbf{v}\|^{2}_{\dot{\mathbf{H}}_{\#}^{s}}=\|\mathbf{v}\|^{2}_{{\mathbf{H}}_{\#}^{s}}\quad\forall\,\mathbf{v}\in\dot{\mathbf{H}}_{\#}^{s}.

(1.20)

Note that the second inequalities in (
1.19
) and (
1.20
) are valid also in the more general cases, i.e., for
\,g\in H_{\#}^{s}
and
\mathbf{v}\in{\mathbf{H}}_{\#}^{s}
, respectively.

We will further need the first Korn inequality

\displaystyle\|\nabla{\mathbf{v}}\|^{2}_{(L_{2\#})^{n\times n}}\leq 2\|\mathbb{E}({\mathbf{v}})\|^{2}_{(L_{2\#})^{n\times n}}\quad\forall\,\mathbf{v}\in{\mathbf{H}}_{\#}^{1}

(1.21)

that can be easily proved by adapting, e.g., the proof in
[
21
, Theorem 10.1]
to the periodic Sobolev space; cf. also
[
26
, Theorem 2.8]
.

Let us also define the Sobolev spaces of divergence-free functions and distributions,

\displaystyle\dot{\mathbf{H}}_{\#\sigma}^{s}

\displaystyle:=\left\{{\bf w}\in\dot{\mathbf{H}}_{\#}^{s}:{{\rm div}}\,{\bf w}=0\right\},\quad s\in{\mathbb{R}},

endowed with the same norm (
1.10
).
Similarly,
{\mathbf{C}}^{\infty}_{\#\sigma}
and
\mathbf{L}_{q\#\sigma}
denote the subspaces of divergence-free vector-functions from

{\mathbf{C}}^{\infty}_{\#}
and
\mathbf{L}_{q\#}
, respectively, etc.

The space
\dot{\mathbf{H}}_{\#\sigma}^{s}
is the Hilbert space with inner product inherited from (
1.11
) and (
1.17
).
In addition, see
[
24
, Section 2]
,

(\dot{\mathbf{H}}_{\#\sigma}^{s})^{*}=\dot{\mathbf{H}}_{\#\sigma}^{-s}.

Not that for any
r,s\in{\mathbb{R}}
the operator

\displaystyle\Lambda_{\#}^{r}:\dot{\mathbf{H}}_{\#\sigma}^{s}\to\dot{\mathbf{H}}_{\#\sigma}^{s-r}

(1.22)

defined as in (
1.14
) is continuous.

Let us also introduce the space

\displaystyle\dot{\mathbf{H}}_{\#g}^{s}

\displaystyle:=\left\{{\bf w}=\nabla q,\ q\in\dot{H}_{\#}^{s+1}\right\},\quad s\in{\mathbb{R}},

endowed with the norm (
1.10
).

The following assertion is produced in
[
24
, Theorem 1]
.

THEOREM 1.1
.

Let
s\in{\mathbb{R}}
and
n\geq 2
.

(a) The space
\dot{\mathbf{H}}_{\#}^{s}
has the Helmholtz-Weyl decomposition,

\dot{\mathbf{H}}_{\#}^{s}=\dot{\mathbf{H}}_{\#g}^{s}\oplus\dot{\mathbf{H}}_{\#\sigma}^{s},

that is, any
\mathbf{F}\in\dot{\mathbf{H}}_{\#}^{s}
can be uniquely represented as

\mathbf{F}=\mathbf{F}_{g}+\mathbf{F}_{\sigma}
, where
\mathbf{F}_{g}\in\dot{\mathbf{H}}_{\#g}^{s}
and

\mathbf{F}_{\sigma}\in\dot{\mathbf{H}}_{\#\sigma}^{s}
.

(b) The spaces
\dot{\mathbf{H}}_{\#g}^{s}
and
\dot{\mathbf{H}}_{\#\sigma}^{s}
are orthogonal subspaces of
\dot{\mathbf{H}}_{\#}^{s}
in the sense of inner product, i.e.,
(\mathbf{w},\mathbf{v})_{H_{\#}^{s}}=0
for any
\mathbf{w}\in\dot{\mathbf{H}}_{\#g}^{s}
and
\mathbf{v}\in\dot{\mathbf{H}}_{\#\sigma}^{-s}
.

(c) The spaces
\dot{\mathbf{H}}_{\#g}^{s}
and
\dot{\mathbf{H}}_{\#\sigma}^{-s}
are orthogonal in the sense of dual product, i.e.,
\langle\mathbf{w},\mathbf{v}\rangle=0
for any
\mathbf{w}\in\dot{\mathbf{H}}_{\#g}^{s}
and

\mathbf{v}\in\dot{\mathbf{H}}_{\#\sigma}^{-s}
.

(d) There exist the bounded orthogonal projector operators
\mathbb{P}_{g}:\dot{\mathbf{H}}_{\#}^{s}\to\dot{\mathbf{H}}_{\#g}^{s}
and
\mathbb{P}_{\sigma}:\dot{\mathbf{H}}_{\#}^{s}\to\dot{\mathbf{H}}_{\#\sigma}^{s}
(the Leray projector), while
\mathbf{F}=\mathbb{P}_{g}\mathbf{F}+\mathbb{P}_{\sigma}\mathbf{F}
for any
\mathbf{F}\in\dot{\mathbf{H}}_{\#}^{s}
.

For the evolution problems we will systematically use the spaces
L_{q}(0,T;H^{s}_{\#})
,
s\in{\mathbb{R}}
,
1\leq q\leq\infty
,
0<T<\infty
, which consists of functions that map
t\in(0,T)
to a function or distributions from
H^{s}_{\#}
.
For
1\leq q<\infty
, the space
L_{q}(0,T;H^{s}_{\#})
is endowed with the norm

\displaystyle\|h\|_{L_{q}(0,T;H^{s}_{\#})}=\left(\int_{0}^{T}\|h(\cdot,t)\|^{q}_{H^{s}_{\#}}dt\right)^{1/q}=\left(\int_{0}^{T}\left[\sum_{\xi\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}}\varrho(\boldsymbol{\xi})^{2s}|\widehat{h}(\boldsymbol{\xi},t)|^{2}\right]^{q/2}dt\right)^{1/q}<\infty,

and for
q=\infty
with the norm

\displaystyle\|h\|_{L_{\infty}(0,T;H^{s}_{\#})}={\rm ess\,sup}_{t\in(0,T)}\|h(\cdot,t)\|_{H^{s}_{\#}}={\rm ess\,sup}_{t\in(0,T)}\left[\sum_{\xi\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}}\varrho(\boldsymbol{\xi})^{2s}|\widehat{h}(\boldsymbol{\xi},t)|^{2}\right]^{1/2}<\infty.

For a function (or distribution)
h(\mathbf{x},t)
, we will use the notation

h^{\prime}(\mathbf{x},t):=\partial_{t}h(\mathbf{x},t):=\dfrac{\partial}{\partial t}h(\mathbf{x},t),\quad h^{(j)}(\mathbf{x},t):=\partial^{j}_{t}h(\mathbf{x},t):=\dfrac{\partial^{j}}{\partial t^{j}}h(\mathbf{x},t)

for the partial derivatives in the time variable
t
.

Let
X
and
Y
be some Hilbert spaces. We will further need the space

\displaystyle W^{1}(0,T;X,Y):=\{u\in L_{2}(0,T;X):u^{\prime}\in L_{2}(0,T;Y)\}

endowed with the norm

\displaystyle\|u\|_{W^{1}(0,T;X,Y)}=(\|u\|_{L_{2}(0,T;X)}^{2}+\|u^{\prime}\|_{L_{2}(0,T;Y)}^{2})^{1/2}.

Spaces of such type are considered in
[
19
, Chapter 1, Section 2.2]
.
We will particularly need the spaces
W^{1}(0,T;H^{s}_{\#},H^{s^{\prime}}_{\#})
and their vector counterparts.

We will also employ the following spaces for
k\in{\mathbb{N}}
, cf., e.g.,
[
19
, Chapter 1, Section 1.3, Remark 1.5]
,

\displaystyle W^{k}(0,T;X):=\{u\in L_{2}(0,T;X):\partial_{t}^{j}{u}\in X,\ j=1,\ldots,k\}

endowed with the norm

\displaystyle\|u\|_{W^{k}(0,T;X)}=\left(\sum_{j=0}^{k}\|\partial_{t}^{j}u\|_{L_{2}(0,T;X)}^{2}\right)^{1/2}.

Unless stated otherwise, we will assume in this paper that all vector and scalar variables are real-valued (however with complex-valued Fourier coefficients).

2
Existence results available for evolution spatially-periodic anisotropic Navier-Stokes problem

Let us consider the following Navier-Stokes problem
for the real-valued unknowns
({\mathbf{u}},p)
,

\displaystyle\mathbf{u}^{\prime}-\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{L}}{\mathbf{u}}+\nabla p+({\mathbf{u}}\cdot\nabla){\mathbf{u}}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{f}\quad\mbox{in }{\mathbb{T}}\times(0,T),

(2.1)

\displaystyle{\rm{div}}\,{\mathbf{u}}

\displaystyle=0\quad\mbox{in }{\mathbb{T}}\times(0,T),

(2.2)

\displaystyle\mathbf{u}(\cdot,0)

\displaystyle=\mathbf{u}^{0}\ \mbox{in }{\mathbb{T}},

(2.3)

with given data

{\mathbf{f}}\in L_{2}(0,T;\dot{\mathbf{H}}_{\#}^{-1})
,
\mathbf{u}^{0}\in\dot{\mathbf{H}}_{\#\sigma}^{0}
.
Note that the time-trace
\mathbf{u}(\cdot,0)
for
\mathbf{u}
solving the weak form of (
2.1
)–(
2.2
) is well defined, see Definition
2.1
and Remark
2.3
.

Let us introduce the bilinear form

\displaystyle a_{{\mathbb{T}}}({\mathbf{u}},{\mathbf{v}})=a_{{\mathbb{T}}}(t;{\mathbf{u}},{\mathbf{v}}):=\left\langle a_{ij}^{\alpha\beta}(\cdot,t)E_{j\beta}({\mathbf{u}}),E_{i\alpha}({\mathbf{v}})\right\rangle_{{\mathbb{T}}}

\displaystyle\,\ \forall\ {\mathbf{u}},{\mathbf{v}}\in\dot{\mathbf{H}}_{\#}^{1}.

(2.4)

By the boundedness condition (
1.5
) and inequality (
1.20
) we have

\displaystyle|a_{{\mathbb{T}}}(t;{\mathbf{u}},{\mathbf{v}})|\leq\|\mathbb{A}\|\|{\mathbb{E}}({\mathbf{u}})\|_{L_{2\#}^{n\times n}}\|{\mathbb{E}}({\bf v})\|_{L_{2\#}^{n\times n}}

\displaystyle\leq\|\mathbb{A}\|\|\nabla{\mathbf{u}}\|_{L_{2\#}^{n\times n}}\|\nabla{\bf v}\|_{L_{2\#}^{n\times n}}

\displaystyle\leq\|\mathbb{A}\|\|{\mathbf{u}}\|_{\dot{\mathbf{H}}_{\#}^{1}}\|{\bf v}\|_{\dot{\mathbf{H}}_{\#}^{1}}\quad\forall\ {\mathbf{u}},{\mathbf{v}}\in\dot{\mathbf{H}}_{\#}^{1}.

(2.5)

If the relaxed ellipticity condition (
1.4
) holds, taking into account the relation
\sum_{i=1}^{n}E_{ii}({\bf w})={\rm div}{\bf w}=0
for
{\bf w}\in{\dot{\mathbf{H}}_{\#\sigma}^{1}}
,
equivalence of the norm
\|\nabla(\cdot)\|_{L_{2\#}^{n\times n}}

to the norm
\|\cdot\|_{\dot{\mathbf{H}}_{\#\sigma}^{1}}
in
\dot{\mathbf{H}}_{\#\sigma}^{1}
, see (
1.20
), and the first Korn inequality (
1.21
),
we obtain

\displaystyle a_{{\mathbb{T}}}(t;{\bf w},{\mathbf{w}})=\left\langle a_{ij}^{\alpha\beta}(\cdot,t)E_{j\beta}({\bf w}),E_{i\alpha}({\bf w})\right\rangle_{{\mathbb{T}}}

\displaystyle\geq C_{\mathbb{A}}^{-1}\|{\mathbb{E}}({\bf w})\|_{L_{2\#}^{n\times n}}^{2}

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{2}C_{\mathbb{A}}^{-1}\|\nabla{\bf w}\|_{L_{2\#}^{n\times n}}^{2}\geq\frac{1}{4}C_{\mathbb{A}}^{-1}\|{\bf w}\|_{\dot{\mathbf{H}}_{\#\sigma}^{1}}^{2}\quad\forall\,{\bf w}\in\dot{\mathbf{H}}_{\#\sigma}^{1}.

(2.6)

Then (
2.5
) and (
2.6
) give

\displaystyle\frac{1}{4}C_{\mathbb{A}}^{-1}\|{\bf w}\|_{\dot{\mathbf{H}}_{\#\sigma}^{1}}^{2}\leq a_{{\mathbb{T}}}(t;{\bf w},{\mathbf{w}})\leq\|\mathbb{A}\|\|{\bf w}\|_{\dot{\mathbf{H}}_{\#\sigma}^{1}}^{2}\quad\forall\,{\bf w}\in\dot{\mathbf{H}}_{\#\sigma}^{1}.

(2.7)

Let us denote

\displaystyle\mathbf{F}:=\mathbf{f}+\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{L}}{\mathbf{u}}-({\mathbf{u}}\cdot\nabla){\mathbf{u}}.

(2.8)

Let
\mathbf{u}\in\dot{\mathbf{H}}_{\#\sigma}^{1}
.
Acting on (
2.1
) by the Leray projector
\mathbb{P}_{\sigma}
and taking into account that

\mathbb{P}_{\sigma}\mathbf{u}^{\prime}=\mathbf{u}^{\prime}
and
\mathbb{P}_{\sigma}\nabla p=\mathbf{0}
, we obtain

\displaystyle\mathbf{u}^{\prime}=\mathbb{P}_{\sigma}\mathbf{F}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}_{\sigma}[\mathbf{f}+\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{L}}{\mathbf{u}}-({\mathbf{u}}\cdot\nabla){\mathbf{u}}]\quad\mbox{in }{\mathbb{T}}\times(0,T).

(2.9)

On the other hand, acting on (
2.1
) by the projector
\mathbb{P}_{g}
and taking into account that

\mathbb{P}_{g}\mathbf{u}^{\prime}=0
and
\mathbb{P}_{g}\nabla p=\nabla p
, we obtain

\displaystyle\nabla p=\mathbb{P}_{g}\mathbf{F}=\mathbb{P}_{g}[\mathbf{f}+\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{L}}{\mathbf{u}}-({\mathbf{u}}\cdot\nabla){\mathbf{u}}]\quad\mbox{in }{\mathbb{T}}\times(0,T).

(2.10)

We use the following definition of weak solution given in
[
24
, Definition 1]
.

DEFINITION 2.1
.

Let
n\geq 2
,
T>0
,
{\mathbf{f}}\in L_{2}(0,T;\dot{\mathbf{H}}_{\#}^{-1})
and

\mathbf{u}^{0}\in\dot{\mathbf{H}}_{\#\sigma}^{0}
.
A function
{\mathbf{u}}\in L_{\infty}(0,T;\dot{\mathbf{H}}_{\#\sigma}^{0})\cap L_{2}(0,T;\dot{\mathbf{H}}_{\#\sigma}^{1})
is called a weak solution of the evolution space-periodic anisotropic Navier-Stokes initial value problem (
2.1
)–(
2.3
) if it solves the initial-variational problem

\displaystyle\left\langle{\mathbf{u}}^{\prime}(\cdot,t)+\mathbb{P}_{\sigma}[({\mathbf{u}}(\cdot,t)\cdot\nabla){\mathbf{u}}(\cdot,t)],{\mathbf{w}}\right\rangle_{{\mathbb{T}}}+a_{{\mathbb{T}}}({\mathbf{u}}(\cdot,t),{\mathbf{w}})

\displaystyle\hskip 160.00024pt=\langle\mathbf{f}(\cdot,t),\mathbf{w}\rangle_{{\mathbb{T}}},\ \text{for a.e. }t\in(0,T),\ \forall\,\mathbf{w}\in\dot{\mathbf{H}}_{\#\sigma}^{1},

(2.11)

\displaystyle\langle{\mathbf{u}}(\cdot,0),\mathbf{w}\rangle_{{\mathbb{T}}}=\langle\mathbf{u}^{0},\mathbf{w}\rangle_{{\mathbb{T}}},\ \forall\,\mathbf{w}\in\dot{\mathbf{H}}_{\#\sigma}^{0}.

(2.12)

The associated pressure
p
is a distribution on
{\mathbb{T}}\times(0,T)
satisfying (
2.1
) in the sense of distributions,
i.e.,

\displaystyle\left\langle{\mathbf{u}}^{\prime}(\cdot,t)+({\mathbf{u}}(\cdot,t)\cdot\nabla){\mathbf{u}}(\cdot,t),{\mathbf{w}}\right\rangle_{{\mathbb{T}}}+a_{{\mathbb{T}}}({\mathbf{u}}(\cdot,t),{\mathbf{w}})

\displaystyle+\langle\nabla p(\cdot,t),{\mathbf{w}}\rangle_{{\mathbb{T}}}

\displaystyle=\langle\mathbf{f}(\cdot,t),\mathbf{w}\rangle_{{\mathbb{T}}},\ \text{for a.e. }t\in(0,T),\ \forall\,\mathbf{w}\in{\mathbf{C}}^{\infty}_{\#}.

(2.13)

The following assertion is proved in
[
24
, Lemma 1]
.

LEMMA 2.2
.

Let
n\geq 2
,
T>0
,
a_{ij}^{\alpha\beta}\in L_{\infty}(0,T;L_{\infty\#})
,
{\mathbf{f}}\in L_{2}(0,T;\dot{\mathbf{H}}_{\#}^{-1})
and

\mathbf{u}^{0}\in\dot{\mathbf{H}}_{\#\sigma}^{0}
.
Let
{\mathbf{u}}\in L_{\infty}(0,T;\dot{\mathbf{H}}_{\#\sigma}^{0})\cap L_{2}(0,T;\dot{\mathbf{H}}_{\#\sigma}^{1})
solve equation (
2.11
).

(i) Then

\displaystyle\mathbf{Du}:={\mathbf{u}}^{\prime}+\mathbb{P}_{\sigma}[({\mathbf{u}}\cdot\nabla){\mathbf{u}}]\in L_{2}(0,T;\dot{\mathbf{H}}_{\#\sigma}^{-1})\quad\text{and}\quad\mathbf{Du}(\cdot,t)\in\dot{\mathbf{H}}_{\#\sigma}^{-1}\text{ for a.e. }t\in[0,T],

(2.14)

while

\displaystyle({\mathbf{u}}\cdot\nabla){\mathbf{u}}\in L_{2}(0,T;\dot{\mathbf{H}}_{\#}^{-n/2})\quad\text{and}\quad({\mathbf{u}}\cdot\nabla){\mathbf{u}}(\cdot,t)\in\dot{\mathbf{H}}_{\#}^{-n/2}\quad\text{ for a.e. }t\in[0,T],

(2.15)

\displaystyle{\mathbf{u}}^{\prime}\in L_{2}(0,T;\dot{\mathbf{H}}_{\#\sigma}^{-n/2})\quad\text{and}\quad{\mathbf{u}}^{\prime}(\cdot,t)\in\dot{\mathbf{H}}_{\#\sigma}^{-n/2}\quad\text{ for a.e. }t\in[0,T],

(2.16)

and hence
{\mathbf{u}}\in W^{1}(\dot{\mathbf{H}}_{\#\sigma}^{1},\dot{\mathbf{H}}_{\#\sigma}^{-n/2})
.

In addition,

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\|{\mathbf{u}}\|_{\dot{\mathbf{H}}^{{-(n-2)/4}}_{\#\sigma}}^{2}=2\langle\Lambda_{\#}^{-n/2}\mathbf{u}^{\prime},\Lambda_{\#}{\mathbf{u}}\rangle_{{\mathbb{T}}}=2\langle\mathbf{u}^{\prime},\Lambda_{\#}^{1-n/2}{\mathbf{u}}\rangle_{{\mathbb{T}}}=2\langle\Lambda_{\#}^{1-n/2}\mathbf{u}^{\prime},{\mathbf{u}}\rangle_{{\mathbb{T}}}

(2.17)

for a.e.
t\in(0,T)
and also in the distribution sense on
(0,T)
.

(ii) Moreover,
\mathbf{u}
is almost everywhere on
[0,T]
equal to a function
\widetilde{\mathbf{u}}\in\mathcal{C}^{0}([0,T];\dot{\mathbf{H}}_{\#\sigma}^{-(n-2)/4})
,
and
\widetilde{\mathbf{u}}
is also
\dot{\mathbf{H}}^{0}_{\#\sigma}
-weakly continuous in time on
[0,T]
, that is,

\lim_{t\to t_{0}}\langle\widetilde{\mathbf{u}}(\cdot,t),\mathbf{w}\rangle_{\mathbb{T}}=\langle\widetilde{\mathbf{u}}(\cdot,t_{0}),\mathbf{w}\rangle_{\mathbb{T}}\quad\forall\,\mathbf{w}\in\mathbf{H}^{0}_{\#},\ \forall\,t_{0}\in[0,T].

(iii) There exists the associated pressure
p\in L_{2}(0,T;\dot{H}_{\#}^{-n/2+1})
that for the given
\mathbf{u}
is the unique solution of equation (
2.1
) in this space.

REMARK 2.3
.

The initial condition (
2.12
) should be understood for the function
\mathbf{u}
re-defined as the function

\widetilde{\mathbf{u}}
that was introduced in Lemma
2.2
(ii) and is
\mathbf{H}^{0}_{\#}
-weakly continuous in time.

The following existence theorem was proved in
[
24
, Theorem 2]
.

THEOREM 2.4

(Existence)
.

Let
n\geq 2
and
T>0
.
Let
a_{ij}^{\alpha\beta}\in L_{\infty}(0,T;L_{\infty\#})

and the relaxed ellipticity condition (
1.4
) hold.
Let
{\mathbf{f}}\in L_{2}(0,T;\dot{\mathbf{H}}_{\#}^{-1})
,
\mathbf{u}^{0}\in\dot{\mathbf{H}}_{\#\sigma}^{0}
.

(i) Then there exists a weak solution

{\mathbf{u}}\in L_{\infty}(0,T;\dot{\mathbf{H}}_{\#\sigma}^{0})\cap L_{2}(0,T;\dot{\mathbf{H}}_{\#\sigma}^{1})

of the anisotropic Navier-Stokes
initial value problem (
2.1
)–(
2.3
) in the sense of Definition
2.1
.
Particularly,

\lim_{t\to 0}\langle{\mathbf{u}}(\cdot,t),\mathbf{v}\rangle_{\mathbb{T}}=\langle{\mathbf{u}}^{0},\mathbf{v}\rangle_{\mathbb{T}}\quad\forall\,\mathbf{v}\in\dot{\mathbf{H}}^{0}_{\#\sigma}.

There exists also the unique pressure
p\in L_{2}(0,T;\dot{H}_{\#}^{-n/2+1})
associated with the obtained
\mathbf{u}
, that is the solution of equation (
2.1
) in
L_{2}(0,T;\dot{H}_{\#}^{-n/2+1})
.

(ii) Moreover,
\mathbf{u}
satisfies the following (strong) energy inequality,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\|{\mathbf{u}}(\cdot,t)\|_{\mathbf{L}_{2\#}}^{2}+\int_{t_{0}}^{t}a_{{\mathbb{T}}}({\mathbf{u}}(\cdot,\tau),{\mathbf{u}}(\cdot,\tau))d\tau\leq\frac{1}{2}\|{\mathbf{u}}(\cdot,t_{0})\|^{2}_{\mathbf{L}_{2\#}}+\int_{t_{0}}^{t}\langle\mathbf{f}(\cdot,\tau),{\mathbf{u}}(\cdot,\tau)\rangle_{{\mathbb{T}}}d\tau

(2.18)

for any
[t_{0},t]\subset[0,T]
. It particularly implies
the standard energy inequality,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\|{\mathbf{u}}(\cdot,t)\|_{\mathbf{L}_{2\#}}^{2}+\int_{0}^{t}a_{{\mathbb{T}}}({\mathbf{u}}(\cdot,\tau),{\mathbf{u}}(\cdot,\tau))d\tau\leq\frac{1}{2}\|{\mathbf{u}}^{0}\|^{2}_{\mathbf{L}_{2\#}}+\int_{0}^{t}\langle\mathbf{f}(\cdot,\tau),{\mathbf{u}}(\cdot,\tau)\rangle_{{\mathbb{T}}}\,d\tau\quad\forall\,t\in[0,T].

(2.19)

3
Serrin-type solutions and their properties

For the isotropic constant-coefficient homogeneous Navier-Stokes equations, it is well know that the weak solution satisfying the famous Prodi-Serrin condition (accommodated here for the periodic vector setting)

\displaystyle\mathbf{u}\in L_{\widetilde{q}}(0,T;\dot{\mathbf{L}}_{\#q})

(3.1)

for some
\widetilde{q}
and
q
such that

\displaystyle\frac{2}{\widetilde{q}}+\frac{n}{q}=1,\quad n<q<\infty,

(3.2)

is unique in the class of weak solutions satisfying the energy inequality, for
n\leq 4
; the energy equality and the regularity results are also proved under the Prodi-Serrin conditions, see, e.g.,
[
31
]
,
[
32
]
,
[
18
, Chapter 1, Theorem 6.9 and Remark 6.8]
,
[
16
, Section 14]
,
[
27
, Section 8.5]
,
[
30
, Theorem 7.17]
,
[
33
, Section 1.5]
.

In this paper we limit ourself to the
L_{2}
-based Sobolev spaces with respect to the spatial variables and hence introduce a corresponding particular counterpart of the class of solutions satisfying the conditions close to (
3.1
)–(
3.2
) and leading to the Serrin-type results.

3.1
Serrin-type solutions and their properties for
n\geq 2

DEFINITION 3.1
.

Let
n\geq 2
,
T>0
,
{\mathbf{f}}\in L_{2}(0,T;\dot{\mathbf{H}}_{\#}^{-1})
and

\mathbf{u}^{0}\in\dot{\mathbf{H}}_{\#\sigma}^{0}
.
If a solution
\mathbf{u}
of the initial-variational problem (
2.11
)–(
2.12
)
belongs to
L_{2}(0,T;\dot{\mathbf{H}}_{\#\sigma}^{n/2})
, we will call it a
Serrin-type solution
.

The inclusion
\mathbf{u}\in L_{2}(0,T;\dot{\mathbf{H}}_{\#\sigma}^{n/2})
can be considered as counterpart of the Prodi-Serrin condition (
3.1
) in
L_{2}
-based Sobolev spaces. Indeed, by the Sobolev embedding theorem,
Theorem
6.6
, we obtain that the following continuous embeddings hold,

\dot{\mathbf{H}}_{\#\sigma}^{n/2}\subset{\mathbf{H}}_{\#}^{n/2}\subset\mathbf{L}_{\#q}
for any
q\in(2,\infty)
.
Hence if
\mathbf{u}\in L_{2}(0,T;\dot{\mathbf{H}}_{\#\sigma}^{n/2})
, then for any
\epsilon>0
there exists
q_{\epsilon}\in(2,\infty)
such that

\mathbf{u}\in L_{\widetilde{q}}(0,T;\mathbf{L}_{\#q_{\epsilon}})

for
\widetilde{q}=2

and

\displaystyle\frac{2}{\widetilde{q}}+\frac{n}{q_{\epsilon}}=1+\epsilon.

(3.3)

Condition (
3.3
) is weaker than condition (
3.2
) by the arbitrarily small
\epsilon>0
, but in spite of this, we will be able to prove that the inclusion
\mathbf{u}\in L_{2}(0,T;\dot{\mathbf{H}}_{\#\sigma}^{n/2})
for the weak solution is sufficient to prove for it the Serrin-type results about the energy equality, uniqueness and regularity, which justifies the chosen Serrin-type solution name.
We will also prove the existence of such solutions, under appropriate conditions.

DEFINITION 3.2
.

Let
n\geq 2
,
T>0
,
{\mathbf{f}}\in L_{2}(0,T;\dot{\mathbf{H}}_{\#}^{-1})
and

\mathbf{u}^{0}\in\dot{\mathbf{H}}_{\#\sigma}^{0}
.
If a solution
\mathbf{u}
of the initial-variational problem (
2.11
)–(
2.12
)
belongs to
L_{2}(0,T;\dot{\mathbf{H}}_{\#\sigma}^{2})
, we will call it a
a strong solution
.

The above definition of the strong solution is a bit weaker than, e.g., in
[
27
, Definition 6.1]
) or
[
18
, Chapter 1, Section 6.7]
, because it does not explicitly require the additional inclusion
\mathbf{u}\in L_{\infty}(0,T;\dot{\mathbf{H}}_{\#\sigma}^{1})
or
\mathbf{u}^{\prime}\in L_{\infty}(0,T;\dot{\mathbf{H}}_{\#\sigma}^{0})
.

REMARK 3.3
.

Definitions
3.1
and
3.2
imply that the strong solutions are also Serrin-type solutions if
n\in\{2,3,4\}
.

The Serrin-type solutions are also strong solutions if
n\geq 4
. Some sufficient conditions for the Serrin-type solution existence are provided in Section
5.2
further on in the paper.

If
n\in\{2,3\}
, then for a Serrin-type solution to be also a strong solution, the Serrin-type solution should have an additional regularity and the sufficient conditions for this are provided by the regularity Theorems and Corollaries in Sections
4
and
5
, with the parameter
r\geq 1
there.

LEMMA 3.4
.

Let
n\geq 2
,
T>0
,
a_{ij}^{\alpha\beta}\in L_{\infty}(0,T;L_{\infty\#})
,

{\mathbf{f}}\in L_{2}(0,T;\dot{\mathbf{H}}_{\#}^{-1})
and

\mathbf{u}^{0}\in\dot{\mathbf{H}}_{\#\sigma}^{0}
.
Let
\mathbf{u}
be a Serrin-type solution.
Then
({\mathbf{u}}\cdot\nabla){\mathbf{u}}\in{L_{2}(0,T;\mathbf{H}_{\#}^{-1})}
,

{\mathbf{u}}^{\prime}\in{L_{2}(0,T;\mathbf{H}_{\#\sigma}^{-1})}
and
hence
{\mathbf{u}}\in W^{1}(\dot{\mathbf{H}}_{\#\sigma}^{n/2},\dot{\mathbf{H}}_{\#\sigma}^{-1})
and

\mathbf{u}\in\mathcal{C}^{0}([0,T];\dot{\mathbf{H}}_{\#\sigma}^{n/4-1/2})\subset\mathcal{C}^{0}([0,T];\dot{\mathbf{H}}_{\#\sigma}^{0}).

Moreover,

\displaystyle\left\langle{\mathbf{u}}^{\prime}(\cdot,t),{\mathbf{w}}\right\rangle_{{\mathbb{T}}}+\left\langle({\mathbf{u}}(\cdot,t)\cdot\nabla){\mathbf{u}}(\cdot,t),{\mathbf{w}}\right\rangle_{{\mathbb{T}}}+a_{{\mathbb{T}}}(t;{\mathbf{u}},{\mathbf{w}})=\langle\mathbf{f}(\cdot,t),\mathbf{w}\rangle_{{\mathbb{T}}},\ \text{for a.e. }t\in(0,T),\ \forall\,\mathbf{w}\in\dot{\mathbf{H}}_{\#\sigma}^{1}.

(3.4)

The unique pressure
p
associated with the obtained
\mathbf{u}
belongs to
L_{2}(0,T;\dot{H}_{\#}^{0})
.

Proof.

By relation (
1.20
), the multiplication Theorem
6.1
(b) and the Sobolev interpolation inequality (
6.16
),

\displaystyle\left\|({\mathbf{u}}\cdot\nabla){\mathbf{u}}\right\|_{\mathbf{H}_{\#}^{-1}}=\left\|\nabla\cdot({\mathbf{u}}\otimes{\mathbf{u}})\right\|_{\mathbf{H}_{\#}^{-1}}\leq\left\|{\mathbf{u}}\otimes{\mathbf{u}})\right\|_{({H}_{\#}^{0})^{n\times n}}^{2}\leq C_{*n}\|\mathbf{u}\|_{{\mathbf{H}}_{\#}^{n/4}}^{2}\leq C_{*n}\|\mathbf{u}\|_{{\mathbf{H}}_{\#}^{0}}\|\mathbf{u}\|_{{\mathbf{H}}_{\#}^{n/2}},

(3.5)

where
C_{*n}=C_{*}(n/4,n/4,n)
.
Hence

\displaystyle\left\|({\mathbf{u}}\cdot\nabla){\mathbf{u}}\right\|_{L_{2}(0,T;\mathbf{H}_{\#}^{-1})}\leq C_{*n}\|\mathbf{u}\|_{L_{\infty}(0,T;{\mathbf{H}}_{\#}^{0})}\|\mathbf{u}\|_{L_{2}(0,T;{\mathbf{H}}_{\#}^{n/2})},

(3.6)

that is,
({\mathbf{u}}\cdot\nabla){\mathbf{u}}\in{L_{2}(0,T;\mathbf{H}_{\#}^{-1})}
and (
2.14
) implies that

{\mathbf{u}}^{\prime}\in{L_{2}(0,T;\mathbf{H}_{\#\sigma}^{-1})}
.
Hence
{\mathbf{u}}\in W^{1}(\dot{\mathbf{H}}_{\#\sigma}^{n/2},\dot{\mathbf{H}}_{\#\sigma}^{-1})
, and Theorem
6.8
implies that

\mathbf{u}\in\mathcal{C}^{0}([0,T];\dot{\mathbf{H}}_{\#\sigma}^{n/4-1/2})\subset\mathcal{C}^{0}([0,T];\dot{\mathbf{H}}_{\#\sigma}^{0})
.
Because of this, equation (
2.11
) for function
{\mathbf{u}}
now reduces to (
3.4
).

To prove the lemma claim about the associated pressure
p
we remark that it satisfies (
2.10
),
where
\mathbf{F}\in L_{2}(0,T;\dot{\mathbf{H}}_{\#}^{-1})

due to the lemma conditions and the inclusion in
({\mathbf{u}}\cdot\nabla){\mathbf{u}}\in{L_{2}(0,T;\mathbf{H}_{\#}^{-1})}
.
By Lemma
6.5
for gradient, with
s=0
, equation (
2.10
) has a unique solution
p
in

L_{2}(0,T;\dot{H}_{\#}^{0})
.
∎

Let us prove, in the variable-coefficient anisotropic setting, the energy equality and solution uniqueness for the Serrin-type solutions.

THEOREM 3.5

(Energy equality for Serrin-type solutions)
.

Let
n\geq 2
,
T>0
,
a_{ij}^{\alpha\beta}\in L_{\infty}(0,T;L_{\infty\#})
,
{\mathbf{f}}\in L_{2}(0,T;\dot{\mathbf{H}}_{\#}^{-1})
and

\mathbf{u}^{0}\in\dot{\mathbf{H}}_{\#\sigma}^{0}
.
If
\mathbf{u}
is a Serrin-type solution
of the initial-variational problem (
2.11
)–(
2.12
),
then the following energy equality holds for any
[t_{0},t]\subset[0,T]
,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\|{\mathbf{u}}(\cdot,t)\|_{\mathbf{L}_{2\#}}^{2}+\int_{t_{0}}^{t}a_{{\mathbb{T}}}(\tau;{\mathbf{u}}(\cdot,\tau),{\mathbf{u}}(\cdot,\tau))d\tau=\frac{1}{2}\|{\mathbf{u}}(\cdot,t_{0})\|^{2}_{\mathbf{L}_{2\#}}+\int_{t_{0}}^{t}\langle\mathbf{f}(\cdot,\tau),{\mathbf{u}}(\cdot,\tau)\rangle_{{\mathbb{T}}}d\tau.

(3.7)

It particularly implies the standard energy equality,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\|{\mathbf{u}}(\cdot,t)\|_{\mathbf{L}_{2\#}}^{2}+\int_{0}^{t}a_{{\mathbb{T}}}(\tau;{\mathbf{u}}(\cdot,\tau),{\mathbf{u}}(\cdot,\tau))d\tau=\frac{1}{2}\|{\mathbf{u}}^{0}\|^{2}_{\mathbf{L}_{2\#}}+\int_{0}^{t}\langle\mathbf{f}(\cdot,\tau),{\mathbf{u}}(\cdot,\tau)\rangle_{{\mathbb{T}}}\,d\tau\quad\forall\,t\in[0,T].

(3.8)

Proof.

By Lemma
3.4
,
the function
{\mathbf{u}}
satisfies equation (
3.4
),
where for a.e.
t\in(0,T)
we can employ
\mathbf{u}
as
\mathbf{w}
to obtain

\displaystyle\left\langle{\mathbf{u}}^{\prime}(\cdot,t),{\mathbf{u}}(\cdot,t)\right\rangle_{{\mathbb{T}}}+\left\langle({\mathbf{u}}(\cdot,t)\cdot\nabla){\mathbf{u}}(\cdot,t),{\mathbf{u}}(\cdot,t)\right\rangle_{{\mathbb{T}}}+a_{{\mathbb{T}}}(t;{\mathbf{u}},{\mathbf{u}}(\cdot,t))=\langle\mathbf{f}(\cdot,t),{\mathbf{u}}(\cdot,t)\rangle_{{\mathbb{T}}},\ \text{for a.e. }t\in(0,T).

(3.9)

Taking into account Lemma
6.9
with
s=1
,
s^{\prime}=-1
for the first dual product and relation (
6.4
) for the second dual product, we get

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\partial_{t}\left\|{\mathbf{u}}(\cdot,t)\right\|_{\mathbf{L}_{2\#}}^{2}+a_{{\mathbb{T}}}(t;{\mathbf{u}},{\mathbf{u}}(\cdot,t))=\langle\mathbf{f}(\cdot,t),{\mathbf{u}}(\cdot,t)\rangle_{{\mathbb{T}}},\ \text{for a.e. }t\in(0,T).

(3.10)

By the inclusions obtained in Lemma
3.4
, each dual product and the bilinear form
a_{\mathbb{T}}
in (
3.9
) and hence in (
3.10
) are integrable in
t
.
After integrating (
3.10
), we obtain (
3.7
)
for a.e.
t_{0}
.

By Lemma
3.4

\mathbf{u}\in\mathcal{C}^{0}([0,T];\dot{\mathbf{H}}_{\#\sigma}^{0}),

while the integrals in (
3.7
) are continuous in
t_{0}
as well.
Then we conclude that the energy equality (
3.7
) holds for any
t_{0}\in[0,T)
, implying also (
3.8
).
∎

THEOREM 3.6

(Uniqueness of Serrin-type solutions)
.

Let
n\geq 2
,
T>0
,
a_{ij}^{\alpha\beta}\in L_{\infty}(0,T;L_{\infty\#})
,
{\mathbf{f}}\in L_{2}(0,T;\dot{\mathbf{H}}_{\#}^{-1})
and

\mathbf{u}^{0}\in\dot{\mathbf{H}}_{\#\sigma}^{0}
.
Let
\mathbf{u}
be a Serrin-type solution
of the initial-variational problem (
2.11
)–(
2.12
) on the interval
[0,T]

and
\mathbf{v}
be any solution
of the initial-variational problem (
2.11
)–(
2.12
) satisfying the energy inequality (
2.19
) on the interval
[0,T]
.
Then
\mathbf{u}=\mathbf{v}
on
[0,T]
.

Proof.

We will here generalise the proof of Theorem 6.10 in
[
27
]
.

By Lemma
3.4
, the function
{\mathbf{u}}
satisfies equation (
3.4
),
where for a.e.
t\in(0,T)
we can employ
\mathbf{v}
as
\mathbf{w}
to obtain

\displaystyle\left\langle{\mathbf{u}}^{\prime}(\cdot,t),{\mathbf{v}}(\cdot,t)\right\rangle_{{\mathbb{T}}}+\left\langle({\mathbf{u}}(\cdot,t)\cdot\nabla){\mathbf{u}}(\cdot,t),{\mathbf{v}}(\cdot,t)\right\rangle_{{\mathbb{T}}}+a_{{\mathbb{T}}}(t;{\mathbf{u}}(\cdot,t),{\mathbf{v}}(\cdot,t))=\langle\mathbf{f}(\cdot,t),{\mathbf{v}}(\cdot,t)\rangle_{{\mathbb{T}}}.

(3.11)

On the other hand, equation (
2.11
) for
\mathbf{v}
with
\mathbf{u}
employed for
\mathbf{w}
can be written for a.e.
t
as

\displaystyle\left\langle{\mathbf{v}}^{\prime}(\cdot,t),{\mathbf{u}}(\cdot,t)\right\rangle_{{\mathbb{T}}}+\left\langle({\mathbf{v}}(\cdot,t)\cdot\nabla){\mathbf{v}}(\cdot,t),{\mathbf{u}}(\cdot,t)\right\rangle_{{\mathbb{T}}}+a_{{\mathbb{T}}}(t;{\mathbf{v}}(\cdot,t),{\mathbf{u}}(\cdot,t))=\langle\mathbf{f}(\cdot,t),{\mathbf{u}}(\cdot,t)\rangle_{{\mathbb{T}}},

(3.12)

where we took into account that

{\mathbf{u}}(t)\in\dot{\mathbf{H}}_{\#\sigma}^{n/2}\subset\dot{\mathbf{H}}_{\#\sigma}^{1}
for a.e.
t
.
Adding equations (
3.11
) and (
3.12
) and integrating in time, we obtain

\int_{0}^{t}\left[\langle{\mathbf{u}}^{\prime}(\cdot,\tau),{\mathbf{v}}(\cdot,\tau)\rangle_{{\mathbb{T}}}+\langle{\mathbf{v}}^{\prime}(\cdot,\tau),{\mathbf{u}}(\cdot,\tau)\rangle_{{\mathbb{T}}}\right]d\tau+2\int_{0}^{t}a_{{\mathbb{T}}}(\tau;{\mathbf{u}}(\cdot,\tau),{\mathbf{v}}(\cdot,\tau))dt\\
+\int_{0}^{t}\left[\langle({\mathbf{u}}(\cdot,\tau)\cdot\nabla){\mathbf{u}}(\cdot,\tau),{\mathbf{v}}(\cdot,\tau)\rangle_{{\mathbb{T}}}+\langle({\mathbf{v}}(\cdot,\tau)\cdot\nabla){\mathbf{v}}(\cdot,\tau),{\mathbf{u}}(\cdot,\tau)\rangle_{{\mathbb{T}}}\right]d\tau\\
=\int_{0}^{t}\langle\mathbf{f}(\cdot,\tau),{\mathbf{u}}(\cdot,\tau)\rangle_{{\mathbb{T}}}d\tau+\int_{0}^{t}\langle\mathbf{f}(\cdot,\tau),{\mathbf{v}}(\cdot,\tau)\rangle_{{\mathbb{T}}}d\tau

(3.13)

By Lemma
2.2
(i),
\mathbf{v}\in W^{1}(0,T;\dot{\mathbf{H}}_{\#\sigma}^{1},\dot{\mathbf{H}}_{\#\sigma}^{-n/2})
and hence by Lemma
6.8
the traces
{\mathbf{v}}(\cdot,0),{\mathbf{v}}(\cdot,t)\in\dot{\mathbf{H}}_{\#\sigma}^{1/2-n/4}
are well defined.
On the other hand, by Lemma
3.4

\mathbf{u}\in W^{1}(0,T;\dot{\mathbf{H}}_{\#\sigma}^{n/2},\dot{\mathbf{H}}_{\#\sigma}^{-1})
and hence by Lemma
6.8
the traces
{\mathbf{u}}(\cdot,0),{\mathbf{u}}(\cdot,t)\in\dot{\mathbf{H}}_{\#\sigma}^{n/4-1/2}
are well defined.
Then due to Lemma
6.9
(ii) with
s=n/2
and
s^{\prime}=-1
,

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}\left[\langle{\mathbf{u}}^{\prime}(\cdot,\tau),{\mathbf{v}}(\cdot,\tau)\rangle_{{\mathbb{T}}}+\langle{\mathbf{v}}^{\prime}(\cdot,\tau),{\mathbf{u}}(\cdot,\tau)\rangle_{{\mathbb{T}}}\right]d\tau=\left\langle{\mathbf{u}}(\cdot,t),{\mathbf{v}}(\cdot,t)\right\rangle_{{\mathbb{T}}}-\left\langle{\mathbf{u}}(\cdot,0),{\mathbf{v}}(\cdot,0)\right\rangle_{{\mathbb{T}}}.

(3.14)

Let us denote
\widetilde{\mathbf{w}}:=\mathbf{u}-\mathbf{v}
.
Since

{\mathbf{u}}(\cdot,0)={\mathbf{v}}(\cdot,0)=\mathbf{u}^{0}\in\dot{\mathbf{H}}_{\#\sigma}^{0}

and
\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v},\widetilde{\mathbf{w}}\in L_{\infty}(0,T;\dot{\mathbf{H}}_{\#\sigma}^{0})
, we obtain

\displaystyle\left\langle{\mathbf{u}}(\cdot,0),{\mathbf{v}}(\cdot,0)\right\rangle_{{\mathbb{T}}}

\displaystyle=\left\|{\mathbf{u}}^{0}\right\|^{2}_{\dot{\mathbf{H}}_{\#\sigma}^{0}}=\left\|{\mathbf{v}}^{0}\right\|^{2}_{\dot{\mathbf{H}}_{\#\sigma}^{0}},

(3.15)

\displaystyle\left\langle{\mathbf{u}}(\cdot,t),{\mathbf{v}}(\cdot,t)\right\rangle_{{\mathbb{T}}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\left\langle{\mathbf{u}}(\cdot,t),{\mathbf{u}}(\cdot,t)\right\rangle_{{\mathbb{T}}}+\frac{1}{2}\left\langle{\mathbf{v}}(\cdot,t),{\mathbf{v}}(\cdot,t)\right\rangle_{{\mathbb{T}}}-\frac{1}{2}\left\langle{\widetilde{\mathbf{w}}}(\cdot,t),{\widetilde{\mathbf{w}}}(\cdot,t)\right\rangle_{{\mathbb{T}}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\left\|{\mathbf{u}}(\cdot,t)\right\|^{2}_{\dot{\mathbf{H}}_{\#\sigma}^{0}}+\frac{1}{2}\left\|{\mathbf{v}}(\cdot,t)\right\|^{2}_{\dot{\mathbf{H}}_{\#\sigma}^{0}}-\frac{1}{2}\left\|{\widetilde{\mathbf{w}}}(\cdot,t)\right\|^{2}_{\dot{\mathbf{H}}_{\#\sigma}^{0}}\text{ for a.e. }t\in(0,T).

(3.16)

Due to (
2.4
),

2a_{{\mathbb{T}}}(\tau;{\mathbf{u}}(\cdot,\tau),{\mathbf{v}}(\cdot,\tau))=a_{{\mathbb{T}}}(\tau;{\mathbf{u}}(\cdot,\tau),{\mathbf{u}}(\cdot,\tau))+a_{{\mathbb{T}}}(\tau;{\mathbf{v}}(\cdot,\tau),{\mathbf{v}}(\cdot,\tau))-a_{{\mathbb{T}}}(\tau;{\widetilde{\mathbf{w}}}(\cdot,\tau),{\widetilde{\mathbf{w}}}(\cdot,\tau)).

By relations (
6.3
) and (
6.4
), we obtain

\displaystyle\langle({\mathbf{u}}(\cdot,\tau)\cdot\nabla)

\displaystyle{\mathbf{u}}(\cdot,\tau),{\mathbf{v}}(\cdot,\tau)\rangle_{{\mathbb{T}}}+\langle({\mathbf{v}}(\cdot,\tau)\cdot\nabla){\mathbf{v}}(\cdot,\tau),{\mathbf{u}}(\cdot,\tau)\rangle_{{\mathbb{T}}}

\displaystyle=\langle({\mathbf{v}}(\cdot,\tau)\cdot\nabla){\mathbf{v}}(\cdot,\tau)-({\mathbf{u}}(\cdot,\tau)\cdot\nabla){\mathbf{v}}(\cdot,\tau),{\mathbf{u}}(\cdot,\tau)\rangle_{{\mathbb{T}}}

\displaystyle=\langle({\widetilde{\mathbf{w}}}(\cdot,\tau)\cdot\nabla){\widetilde{\mathbf{w}}}(\cdot,\tau)-({\widetilde{\mathbf{w}}}(\cdot,\tau)\cdot\nabla){\mathbf{u}}(\cdot,\tau),{\mathbf{u}}(\cdot,\tau)\rangle_{{\mathbb{T}}}=\langle({\widetilde{\mathbf{w}}}(\cdot,\tau)\cdot\nabla){\widetilde{\mathbf{w}}}(\cdot,\tau),{\mathbf{u}}(\cdot,\tau)\rangle_{{\mathbb{T}}}.

(3.17)

Substituting (
3.14
)–(
3.17
) into (
3.13
), we get

\frac{1}{2}\left\|\widetilde{\mathbf{w}}(\cdot,t)\right\|^{2}_{\dot{\mathbf{H}}_{\#\sigma}^{0}}+\int_{0}^{t}a_{{\mathbb{T}}}(\tau;\widetilde{\mathbf{w}}(\cdot,\tau),\widetilde{\mathbf{w}}(\cdot,\tau))dt-\int_{0}^{t}\langle({\widetilde{\mathbf{w}}}(\cdot,\tau)\cdot\nabla){\widetilde{\mathbf{w}}}(\cdot,\tau),{\mathbf{u}}(\cdot,\tau)\rangle_{{\mathbb{T}}}d\tau\\
=A(\mathbf{u};t)+A(\mathbf{v};t)\text{ for a.e. }t\in(0,T).

(3.18)

Here

\displaystyle A(\mathbf{u}):=\frac{1}{2}\left\|{\mathbf{u}}(\cdot,t)\right\|^{2}_{\dot{\mathbf{H}}_{\#\sigma}^{0}}+\int_{0}^{t}a_{{\mathbb{T}}}(\tau;{\mathbf{u}}(\cdot,\tau),{\mathbf{u}}(\cdot,\tau))dt-\frac{1}{2}\left\|{\mathbf{u}}^{0}\right\|^{2}_{\dot{\mathbf{H}}_{\#\sigma}^{0}}-\int_{0}^{t}\langle\mathbf{f}(\cdot,\tau),{\mathbf{u}}(\cdot,\tau)\rangle_{{\mathbb{T}}}d\tau=0

(3.19)

by the energy equality condition (
3.8
) for
\mathbf{u}
, while

\displaystyle A(\mathbf{v}):=\frac{1}{2}\left\|{\mathbf{v}}(\cdot,t)\right\|^{2}_{\dot{\mathbf{H}}_{\#\sigma}^{0}}+\int_{0}^{t}a_{{\mathbb{T}}}(\tau;{\mathbf{v}}(\cdot,\tau),{\mathbf{v}}(\cdot,\tau))dt-\frac{1}{2}\left\|{\mathbf{v}}^{0}\right\|^{2}_{\dot{\mathbf{H}}_{\#\sigma}^{0}}-\int_{0}^{t}\langle\mathbf{f}(\cdot,\tau),{\mathbf{v}}(\cdot,\tau)\rangle_{{\mathbb{T}}}d\tau\leq 0

(3.20)

by the energy inequality condition (
2.19
) for
\mathbf{v}
.

Taking into account inequality (
2.6
) for the quadratic form
a
, (
3.18
)–(
3.20
) imply

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\left\|\widetilde{\mathbf{w}}(\cdot,t)\right\|^{2}_{\dot{\mathbf{H}}_{\#\sigma}^{0}}+\frac{1}{4}C_{\mathbb{A}}^{-1}\int_{0}^{t}\|\widetilde{\mathbf{w}}(\cdot,\tau)\|^{2}_{\dot{\mathbf{H}}_{\#\sigma}^{1}}dt\leq\int_{0}^{t}\left|\langle({\widetilde{\mathbf{w}}}(\cdot,\tau)\cdot\nabla){\widetilde{\mathbf{w}}}(\cdot,\tau),{\mathbf{u}}(\cdot,\tau)\rangle_{{\mathbb{T}}}\right|d\tau.

(3.21)

By the multiplication Theorem
6.1
(b), the Sobolev interpolation inequality (
6.16
) and Young’s inequality, we obtain

\left|\langle({\widetilde{\mathbf{w}}}(\cdot,\tau)\cdot\nabla){\widetilde{\mathbf{w}}}(\cdot,\tau),{\mathbf{u}}(\cdot,\tau)\rangle_{{\mathbb{T}}}\right|\leq\|({\widetilde{\mathbf{w}}}(\cdot,\tau)\cdot\nabla){\widetilde{\mathbf{w}}}(\cdot,\tau)\|_{\dot{\mathbf{H}}_{\#\sigma}^{-n/2}}\|{\mathbf{u}}(\cdot,\tau)\|_{\dot{\mathbf{H}}_{\#\sigma}^{n/2}}\\
\leq\|\nabla\cdot[{\widetilde{\mathbf{w}}}(\cdot,\tau)\otimes{\widetilde{\mathbf{w}}}(\cdot,\tau)]\|_{\dot{\mathbf{H}}_{\#\sigma}^{-n/2}}\|{\mathbf{u}}(\cdot,\tau)\|_{\dot{\mathbf{H}}_{\#\sigma}^{n/2}}\leq\|{\widetilde{\mathbf{w}}}(\cdot,\tau)\otimes{\widetilde{\mathbf{w}}}(\cdot,\tau)]\|_{({H}_{\#}^{-n/2+1})^{n\times n}}\|{\mathbf{u}}(\cdot,\tau)\|_{\dot{\mathbf{H}}_{\#\sigma}^{n/2}}\\
\leq C_{*}\|{\widetilde{\mathbf{w}}}(\cdot,\tau)\|^{2}_{\dot{\mathbf{H}}_{\#\sigma}^{1/2}}\|{\mathbf{u}}(\cdot,\tau)\|_{\dot{\mathbf{H}}_{\#\sigma}^{n/2}}\leq C_{*}\|{\widetilde{\mathbf{w}}}(\cdot,\tau)\|_{\dot{\mathbf{H}}_{\#\sigma}^{0}}\|{\widetilde{\mathbf{w}}}(\cdot,\tau)\|_{\dot{\mathbf{H}}_{\#\sigma}^{1}}\|{\mathbf{u}}(\cdot,\tau)\|_{\dot{\mathbf{H}}_{\#\sigma}^{n/2}}\\
\leq\frac{1}{4}C_{\mathbb{A}}^{-1}\|{\widetilde{\mathbf{w}}}(\cdot,\tau)\|^{2}_{\dot{\mathbf{H}}_{\#\sigma}^{1}}+C_{\mathbb{A}}C_{*}^{2}\|{\widetilde{\mathbf{w}}}(\cdot,\tau)\|^{2}_{\dot{\mathbf{H}}_{\#\sigma}^{0}}\|{\mathbf{u}}(\cdot,\tau)\|^{2}_{\dot{\mathbf{H}}_{\#\sigma}^{n/2}},

(3.22)

where
C_{*}:=C_{*}(1/2,1/2,n)
if from Theorem
6.1
(b).
Implementing (
3.22
) in (
3.21
), we obtain

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\left\|\widetilde{\mathbf{w}}(\cdot,t)\right\|^{2}_{\dot{\mathbf{H}}_{\#\sigma}^{0}}\leq C_{\mathbb{A}}C_{*}^{2}\int_{0}^{t}\|{\widetilde{\mathbf{w}}}(\cdot,\tau)\|^{2}_{\dot{\mathbf{H}}_{\#\sigma}^{0}}\|{\mathbf{u}}(\cdot,\tau)\|^{2}_{\dot{\mathbf{H}}_{\#\sigma}^{n/2}}d\tau.

(3.23)

Since

\int_{0}^{T}\|{\widetilde{\mathbf{w}}}(\cdot,\tau)\|^{2}_{\dot{\mathbf{H}}_{\#\sigma}^{0}}\|{\mathbf{u}}(\cdot,\tau)\|^{2}_{\dot{\mathbf{H}}_{\#\sigma}^{n/2}}d\tau\leq\|{\widetilde{\mathbf{w}}}\|^{2}_{L_{\infty}(0,T;\dot{\mathbf{H}}_{\#\sigma}^{0})}\|\mathbf{u}\|^{2}_{L_{2}(0,T;\dot{\mathbf{H}}_{\#\sigma}^{n/2})}<\infty,

we can employ to (
3.23
) the integral Gronwal’s inequality from Lemma
6.13
to conclude that

\|{\widetilde{\mathbf{w}}}(\cdot,\tau)\|_{\dot{\mathbf{H}}_{\#\sigma}^{0}}=0
.
∎

3.2
Serrin-type property of the two-dimensional weak solution

By Definitions
2.1
and
3.1
, any week solution of the evolution space-periodic anisotropic Navier-Stokes initial value problem (
2.1
)–(
2.3
) is a Serrin-type solution for
n=2
.
Then Lemmas
2.2
and
3.4
along with Theorems
3.5
and
3.6
lead to the following results for any
T>0
and arbitrarily large data (unlike the higher dimensions discussed further on).

THEOREM 3.7
.

Let
n=2
,
T>0
,

a_{ij}^{\alpha\beta}\in L_{\infty}(0,T;L_{\infty\#})

and the relaxed ellipticity condition (
1.4
) hold.
Let
{\mathbf{f}}\in L_{2}(0,T;\dot{\mathbf{H}}_{\#}^{-1})
,
\mathbf{u}^{0}\in\dot{\mathbf{H}}_{\#\sigma}^{0}
.

Then the solution
\mathbf{u}\in L_{\infty}(0,T_{*};\dot{\mathbf{H}}_{\#\sigma}^{0})\cap L_{2}(0,T_{*};\dot{\mathbf{H}}_{\#\sigma}^{1})
of the anisotropic Navier-Stokes
initial value problem (
2.1
)–(
2.3
) obtained in Theorem
2.4
is of Serrin-type and hence
({\mathbf{u}}\cdot\nabla){\mathbf{u}}\in{L_{2}(0,T;\mathbf{H}_{\#}^{-1})}
,

{\mathbf{u}}\in W^{1}(\dot{\mathbf{H}}_{\#\sigma}^{1},\dot{\mathbf{H}}_{\#\sigma}^{-1})
,

\mathbf{u}
is almost everywhere on
[0,T]
equal to a function belonging to
\mathcal{C}^{0}([0,T];\dot{\mathbf{H}}_{\#\sigma}^{0})
and

\lim_{t\to 0}\|\mathbf{u}(\cdot,t)-{\mathbf{u}}^{0}\|_{\dot{\mathbf{H}}_{\#\sigma}^{0}}=0.

In addition,

\displaystyle\left\langle{\mathbf{u}}^{\prime}(\cdot,t),{\mathbf{w}}\right\rangle_{{\mathbb{T}}}+\left\langle({\mathbf{u}}(\cdot,t)\cdot\nabla){\mathbf{u}}(\cdot,t),{\mathbf{w}}\right\rangle_{{\mathbb{T}}}+a_{{\mathbb{T}}}(t;{\mathbf{u}},{\mathbf{w}})=\langle\mathbf{f}(\cdot,t),\mathbf{w}\rangle_{{\mathbb{T}}},\ \text{for a.e. }t\in(0,T),\ \forall\,\mathbf{w}\in\dot{\mathbf{H}}_{\#\sigma}^{1},

and the following energy equality holds,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\|{\mathbf{u}}(\cdot,t)\|_{\mathbf{L}_{2\#}}^{2}+\int_{0}^{t}a_{{\mathbb{T}}}(\tau;{\mathbf{u}}(\cdot,\tau),{\mathbf{u}}(\cdot,\tau))d\tau=\frac{1}{2}\|{\mathbf{u}}^{0}\|^{2}_{\mathbf{L}_{2\#}}+\int_{0}^{t}\langle\mathbf{f}(\cdot,\tau),{\mathbf{u}}(\cdot,\tau)\rangle_{{\mathbb{T}}}\,d\tau\quad\forall\,t\in[0,T].

Moreover, the solution
\mathbf{u}
is unique in the class of solutions from

L_{\infty}(0,T;\dot{\mathbf{H}}_{\#\sigma}^{0})\cap L_{2}(0,T;\dot{\mathbf{H}}_{\#\sigma}^{1})

satisfying the energy inequality (
2.19
).
The unique pressure
p
associated with the obtained
\mathbf{u}
belongs to
L_{2}(0,T;\dot{H}_{\#}^{0})
.

4
Serrin-type solution existence and regularity for constant anisotropic viscosity coefficients

In this section we analyse the existence and regularity of Serrin-type solutions for any
n\geq 2
in the anisotropic variable-coefficient case.
This gives a generalisation of Theorem 10.1 in
[
27
]
, where similar results were obtained for
n=3
, for the smoothness index
r=1/2
, and for the isotropic constant viscosity coefficients.

4.1
Vector heat equation

Let us first consider the spatially periodic Cauchy problem for the (vector) heat equation,

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\mathbf{v}-\Delta{\mathbf{v}}=\mathbf{0}\quad

\displaystyle\mbox{in }{\mathbb{T}}\times(0,\infty),

(4.1)

\displaystyle\mathbf{v}(\cdot,0)=\mathbf{u}^{0}\quad

\displaystyle\mbox{in }{\mathbb{T}}.

(4.2)

Its periodic solution can be written as

\displaystyle\mathbf{v}(\mathbf{x},t)=(K{\mathbf{u}}^{0})(\mathbf{x},t):=\sum_{\boldsymbol{\xi}\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}}\widehat{\mathbf{u}}^{0}(\boldsymbol{\xi})e^{-(2\pi|\boldsymbol{\xi}|)^{2}t+2\pi i\mathbf{x}\cdot\boldsymbol{\xi}}.

(4.3)

If
{\rm div}\,{\mathbf{u}}^{0}(\mathbf{x})=0
and
\widehat{\mathbf{u}}^{0}(\mathbf{0})=\mathbf{0}
then

{\rm div}\,{\mathbf{v}}(\mathbf{x},t)=0
and
\widehat{\mathbf{v}}(\mathbf{0},t)=\mathbf{0}
.
Particularly, let us assume that
\mathbf{u}^{0}\in\dot{\mathbf{H}}_{\#\sigma}^{r}
for some
r\in{\mathbb{R}}
. Then taking dual product of the both sides of equation (
4.1
) with
\Lambda^{2r}_{\#}{\mathbf{v}}
gives

\displaystyle\langle\partial_{t}\Lambda^{r}_{\#}{\mathbf{v}},\Lambda^{r}_{\#}{\mathbf{v}}\rangle_{{\mathbb{T}}}+\langle{\nabla\Lambda^{r}_{\#}\mathbf{v}},\nabla\Lambda^{r}_{\#}{\bf v}\rangle_{\mathbb{T}}=\mathbf{0},\

implying

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\frac{d}{dt}\left\|{\mathbf{v}}\right\|_{\mathbf{H}^{r}_{\#}}^{2}+\|\nabla{\mathbf{v}}\|_{({H}_{\#}^{r})^{n\times n}}^{2}=0.

After integration this gives the energy-type equality

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\left\|{\mathbf{v}}(\cdot,t)\right\|_{\mathbf{H}^{r}_{\#}}^{2}+\int_{0}^{t}\|\nabla{\mathbf{v}}\|_{({H}_{\#}^{r})^{n\times n}}^{2}d\tau=\frac{1}{2}\left\|{\mathbf{u}}^{0}\right\|_{\mathbf{H}^{r}_{\#}}^{2},\quad t\geq 0.

(4.4)

The solution representation (
4.3
) and the norm definition (
1.10
) imply that

\displaystyle\left\|{\mathbf{v}}(\cdot,t)\right\|_{\mathbf{H}^{r}_{\#}}^{2}\leq\left\|{\mathbf{u}}^{0}\right\|_{\mathbf{H}^{r}_{\#}}^{2}\quad t\geq 0,

(4.5)

On the other hand, (
1.20
) and (
4.4
) lead to

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}\left\|K{\mathbf{u}}^{0}\right\|_{\dot{\mathbf{H}}^{r+1}_{\#}}^{2}d\tau=\int_{0}^{t}\|\mathbf{v}\|_{\dot{\mathbf{H}}_{\#}^{r+1}}^{2}d\tau\leq 2\int_{0}^{t}\|\nabla{\mathbf{v}}\|_{({H}_{\#}^{r})^{n\times n}}^{2}d\tau\leq\left\|{\mathbf{u}}^{0}\right\|_{\mathbf{H}^{r}_{\#}}^{2},\quad t\geq 0.

(4.6)

Estimates (
4.5
) and (
4.6
)
mean that
{\mathbf{v}}\in{L_{\infty}(0,T;\dot{\mathbf{H}}^{r}_{\#\sigma})}\cup{L_{2}(0,T;\dot{\mathbf{H}}_{\#\sigma}^{r+1})}

for any
T>0

and

\displaystyle\left\|{\mathbf{v}}\right\|_{L_{\infty}(0,T;\mathbf{H}^{r}_{\#})}^{2}=\left\|K{\mathbf{u}}^{0}\right\|_{L_{\infty}(0,T;\mathbf{H}^{r}_{\#})}^{2}\leq\left\|{\mathbf{u}}^{0}\right\|_{\mathbf{H}^{r}_{\#}}^{2},

(4.7)

\displaystyle\|{\mathbf{v}}\|_{L_{2}(0,T;\mathbf{H}_{\#}^{r+1})}^{2}=\left\|K{\mathbf{u}}^{0}\right\|_{L_{2}(0,T;\mathbf{H}^{r+1}_{\#})}^{2}\leq\left\|{\mathbf{u}}^{0}\right\|_{\mathbf{H}^{r}_{\#}}^{2},\quad\forall\,T>0.

(4.8)

This implies that
the operator
K:\dot{\mathbf{H}}^{r}_{\#\sigma}\to L_{\infty}(0,\infty;\dot{\mathbf{H}}^{r}_{\#\sigma})\cup L_{2}(0,\infty;\dot{\mathbf{H}}_{\#\sigma}^{r+1})
is continuous.

4.2
Preliminary results
for constant anisotropic viscosity coefficients

For some
n\geq 2
,
r\geq{n}/{2}-1
, and
T>0
, let the coefficients

a_{ij}^{\alpha\beta}
be constant and the relaxed ellipticity condition (
1.4
) hold.
Let also
{\mathbf{f}}\in L_{2}(0,T;\dot{\mathbf{H}}_{\#}^{r-1})
and
\mathbf{u}^{0}\in\dot{\mathbf{H}}_{\#\sigma}^{r}
.

Let us employ, as usual, the Galerkin approximation, with the sequence

\{\mathbf{w}_{\ell}\}\subset\dot{\mathbf{C}}^{\infty}_{\#\sigma}
of eigenfunctions of the Bessel potential operator
\Lambda_{\#}
in
\dot{\mathbf{H}}^{0}_{\#\sigma}
, corresponding to eigenvalues
\lambda_{\ell}
and constituting an orthonormal basis in
\dot{\mathbf{H}}_{\#\sigma}^{0}
, see Section
6.3
.
They also constitute an orthogonal basis in
\dot{\mathbf{H}}_{\#\sigma}^{r}
and
\dot{\mathbf{H}}_{\#\sigma}^{r+1}
, see Theorem
6.4
.
Let us construct the
m
-term approximation to
\mathbf{u}^{0}
,

\mathbf{u}_{m}^{0}:=P_{m}\mathbf{u}_{m}^{0}=\sum_{\ell=1}^{m}\langle\mathbf{u}^{0},\mathbf{w}_{\ell}\rangle_{\mathbb{T}}\mathbf{w}_{\ell},

where
P_{m}
is the orthogonal projector from
{\mathbf{H}}^{r}_{\#\sigma}
to
{\rm Span}\{\mathbf{w}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{w}_{m}\}
,
cf. (
6.13
), that converges in

\dot{\mathbf{H}}_{\#\sigma}^{0}
,
\dot{\mathbf{H}}_{\#\sigma}^{r}
and

\dot{\mathbf{H}}_{\#\sigma}^{r+1}
as
m\to\infty
.
Due to the basis orthogonality, we have the inequalities

\displaystyle\|\mathbf{u}^{0}_{m}\|_{\dot{\mathbf{H}}_{\#\sigma}^{0}}\leq\|\mathbf{u}^{0}\|_{\dot{\mathbf{H}}_{\#\sigma}^{0}},\quad\|\mathbf{u}^{0}_{m}\|_{\dot{\mathbf{H}}_{\#\sigma}^{r}}\leq\|\mathbf{u}^{0}\|_{\dot{\mathbf{H}}_{\#\sigma}^{r}},\quad\|\mathbf{u}^{0}_{m}\|_{\dot{\mathbf{H}}_{\#\sigma}^{r+1}}\leq\|\mathbf{u}^{0}\|_{\dot{\mathbf{H}}_{\#\sigma}^{r+1}}.

Let
\{\mathbf{u}_{m}\}
be the sequence employed to prove Theorem 2 in
[
24
]
, given here as Theorem
2.4
.
The sequence
\{\mathbf{u}_{m}\}
converges to a solution

\mathbf{u}\in L_{\infty}(0,T;\dot{\mathbf{H}}_{\#\sigma}^{0})\cap L_{2}(0,T;\dot{\mathbf{H}}_{\#\sigma}^{1})
of the initial-variational problem (
2.11
)–(
2.12
) weakly in
L_{2}(0,T;\dot{\mathbf{H}}_{\#\sigma}^{1})
, weakly-star in
L_{\infty}(0,T;\dot{\mathbf{H}}_{\#\sigma}^{0})
and strongly in
L_{2}(0,T;\dot{\mathbf{H}}_{\#\sigma}^{0})
.
Particularly,

\mathbf{u}_{m}(\mathbf{x},t)=\sum_{\ell=1}^{m}\eta_{\ell,m}(t)\mathbf{w}_{\ell}

and solves the following nonlinear ODE problem
from Theorem 2 in
[
24
]
,

\displaystyle\langle\partial_{t}{\mathbf{u}}_{m},{\mathbf{w}}_{k}\rangle_{{\mathbb{T}}}+a_{{\mathbb{T}}}(t;{\mathbf{u}}_{m},{\bf w}_{k})+\langle({\mathbf{u}}_{m}\cdot\nabla){\mathbf{u}}_{m},{\bf w}_{k}\rangle_{{\mathbb{T}}}=\langle\mathbf{f},\mathbf{w}_{k}\rangle_{{\mathbb{T}}},\ \text{a.e. }t\in(0,T),\ \forall\,k\in\{1,\ldots,m\},

(4.9)

\displaystyle\langle\mathbf{u}_{m},\mathbf{w}_{k}\rangle_{{\mathbb{T}}}(\cdot,0)=\langle\mathbf{u}^{0},\mathbf{w}_{k}\rangle_{{\mathbb{T}}},\quad\forall\,k\in\{1,\ldots,m\}.

(4.10)

Similarly to the proof of Theorem 10.1 in
[
27
]
,
let us define

\mathbf{v}_{m}(\mathbf{x},t):=P_{m}\mathbf{v}=\sum_{k=1}^{m}\langle\mathbf{v},\mathbf{w}_{k}\rangle_{\mathbb{T}}\mathbf{w}_{k}

for
\mathbf{v}
given by (
4.3
).
Acting by the projector
P_{m}
on (
4.1
)–(
4.2
) and then taking the dual product with
\mathbf{w}_{k}
, we obtain that for any
m>1
,
\mathbf{v}_{m}

solves the initial value ODE problem

\displaystyle\langle\partial_{t}{\mathbf{v}}_{m},{\mathbf{w}}_{k}\rangle_{{\mathbb{T}}}+\langle{\nabla\mathbf{v}}_{m},\nabla{\bf w}_{k}\rangle_{\mathbb{T}}=\mathbf{0},\ \forall\,t\in(0,T),\ \forall\,k\in\{1,\ldots,m\},

(4.11)

\displaystyle\langle\mathbf{v}_{m},\mathbf{w}_{k}\rangle_{{\mathbb{T}}}(\cdot,0)=\langle\mathbf{u}^{0},\mathbf{w}_{k}\rangle_{{\mathbb{T}}},\quad\forall\,k\in\{1,\ldots,m\},

(4.12)

and by (
4.7
) satisfies the estimates

\displaystyle\left\|{\mathbf{v}}_{m}\right\|_{L_{\infty}(0,T;\mathbf{H}^{r}_{\#})}^{2}\leq\left\|{\mathbf{v}}\right\|_{L_{\infty}(0,T;\mathbf{H}^{r}_{\#})}^{2}\leq\left\|{\mathbf{u}}^{0}\right\|_{\mathbf{H}^{r}_{\#}}^{2},\quad\forall\,T>0,

(4.13)

\displaystyle\|{\mathbf{v}_{m}}\|_{L_{2}(0,T;\mathbf{H}_{\#}^{r+1})}^{2}\leq\|{\mathbf{v}}\|_{L_{2}(0,T;\mathbf{H}_{\#}^{r+1})}^{2}\leq\left\|{\mathbf{u}}^{0}\right\|_{\mathbf{H}^{r}_{\#}}^{2},\quad\forall\,T>0.

(4.14)

To reduce the problem (
4.9
)-(
4.10
) to the one with zero initial conditions, let us represent
\mathbf{u}_{m}=\mathbf{v}_{m}+\widetilde{\mathbf{u}}_{m}
.
Then due to (
4.9
)–(
4.10
), the auxiliary function

\widetilde{\mathbf{u}}_{m}(\mathbf{x},t)=\sum_{\ell=1}^{m}\widetilde{\eta}_{\ell,m}(t)\mathbf{w}_{\ell}

satisfies the ODE problem

\displaystyle\langle\partial_{t}\widetilde{\mathbf{u}}_{m},{\mathbf{w}}_{k}\rangle_{{\mathbb{T}}}+a_{{\mathbb{T}}}(t;\widetilde{\mathbf{u}}_{m},{\bf w}_{k})+\langle({\mathbf{u}}_{m}\cdot\nabla){\mathbf{u}}_{m},{\bf w}_{k}\rangle_{{\mathbb{T}}}

\displaystyle\hskip 100.00015pt=\langle\mathbf{f},\mathbf{w}_{k}\rangle_{{\mathbb{T}}}+\langle{\nabla\mathbf{v}}_{m},\nabla{\bf w}_{k}\rangle_{\mathbb{T}}-a_{{\mathbb{T}}}(t;{\mathbf{v}}_{m},{\bf w}_{k}),\ \forall\,k\in\{1,\ldots,m\},

(4.15)

\displaystyle\langle\widetilde{\mathbf{u}}_{m},\mathbf{w}_{k}\rangle_{{\mathbb{T}}}(\cdot,0)=\mathbf{0},\quad\forall\,k\in\{1,\ldots,m\}.

(4.16)

After multiplying by
\lambda^{2r}_{k}
and taking into account the property
\Lambda^{2r}_{\#}\mathbf{w}_{k}=\lambda^{2r}_{k}\mathbf{w}_{k}
, relation (
2.4
), and that the operator
\Lambda^{r}_{\#}
commutate with operators
\nabla
and
E_{j\beta}
, equation (
4.15
) leads to

\langle\partial_{t}\widetilde{\boldsymbol{u}}_{m},\Lambda^{2r}_{\#}\mathbf{w}_{k}\rangle_{\mathbb{T}}+\left\langle E_{j\beta}(\widetilde{\mathbf{u}}_{m}),a_{ij}^{\alpha\beta}\Lambda^{r}_{\#}E_{i\alpha}(\Lambda^{r}_{\#}{\mathbf{w}}_{k})\right\rangle_{{\mathbb{T}}}+\langle({\mathbf{u}}_{m}\cdot\nabla){\mathbf{u}}_{m},\Lambda^{2r}_{\#}{\bf w}_{k}\rangle_{{\mathbb{T}}}\\
\hskip 10.00002pt=\langle\mathbf{f},\Lambda^{2r}_{\#}\mathbf{w}_{k}\rangle_{\mathbb{T}}+\langle{\nabla\mathbf{v}}_{m},\Lambda^{r}_{\#}\nabla\Lambda^{r}_{\#}{\bf w}_{k}\rangle_{\mathbb{T}}-\left\langle E_{j\beta}({\bf v}_{m}),a_{ij}^{\alpha\beta}\Lambda^{r}_{\#}E_{i\alpha}(\Lambda^{r}_{\#}{\mathbf{w}}_{k})\right\rangle_{{\mathbb{T}}},\quad\forall\,k\in\{1,\ldots,m\}.

(4.17)

These equations can be re-written as

\langle\partial_{t}\Lambda^{r}_{\#}\widetilde{\boldsymbol{u}}_{m},\Lambda^{r}_{\#}\mathbf{w}_{k}\rangle_{\mathbb{T}}+\left\langle a_{ij}^{\alpha\beta}E_{j\beta}(\Lambda^{r}_{\#}\widetilde{\mathbf{u}}_{m}),E_{i\alpha}(\Lambda^{r}_{\#}{\mathbf{w}}_{k})\right\rangle_{{\mathbb{T}}}+\langle\Lambda^{r-1}_{\#}[({\mathbf{u}}_{m}\cdot\nabla){\mathbf{u}}_{m}],\Lambda^{r+1}_{\#}{\bf w}_{k}\rangle_{{\mathbb{T}}}\\
=\langle\Lambda^{r-1}_{\#}\mathbf{f},\Lambda^{r+1}_{\#}\mathbf{w}_{k}\rangle_{\mathbb{T}}+\langle{\nabla\Lambda^{r}_{\#}\mathbf{v}}_{m},\nabla\Lambda^{r}_{\#}{\bf w}_{k}\rangle_{\mathbb{T}}-\left\langle E_{j\beta}({\bf v}_{m}),a_{ij}^{\alpha\beta}\Lambda^{r}_{\#}E_{i\alpha}(\Lambda^{r}_{\#}{\mathbf{w}}_{k})\right\rangle_{{\mathbb{T}}}\quad\forall\,k\in\{1,\ldots,m\}.

(4.18)

Multiplying equations in (
4.18
) by
\widetilde{\eta}_{k,m}(t)
and summing them up over
k\in\{1,\ldots,m\}
, we obtain

\frac{1}{2}\partial_{t}\left\|\Lambda^{r}_{\#}\widetilde{\mathbf{u}}_{m}\right\|_{\mathbf{H}^{0}_{\#}}^{2}+a_{{\mathbb{T}}}(\Lambda^{r}_{\#}\widetilde{\mathbf{u}}_{m},\Lambda^{r}_{\#}\widetilde{\mathbf{u}}_{m})=\langle\Lambda^{r-1}_{\#}\mathbf{f},\Lambda^{r+1}_{\#}\widetilde{\mathbf{u}}_{m}\rangle_{\mathbb{T}}+\langle{\nabla\Lambda^{r}_{\#}\mathbf{v}}_{m},\nabla\Lambda^{r}_{\#}\widetilde{\mathbf{u}}_{m}\rangle_{\mathbb{T}}\\
-\left\langle E_{j\beta}({\bf v}_{m}),a_{ij}^{\alpha\beta}\Lambda^{r}_{\#}E_{i\alpha}(\Lambda^{r}_{\#}\widetilde{\mathbf{u}}_{m})\right\rangle_{{\mathbb{T}}}-\langle\Lambda^{r-1}_{\#}[({\mathbf{u}}_{m}\cdot\nabla){\mathbf{u}}_{m}],\Lambda^{r+1}_{\#}\widetilde{\mathbf{u}}_{m}\rangle_{{\mathbb{T}}}.

(4.19)

From (
2.7
) we have

\displaystyle a_{{\mathbb{T}}}(\Lambda^{r}_{\#}\widetilde{\mathbf{u}}_{m},\Lambda^{r}_{\#}\widetilde{\mathbf{u}}_{m})

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{4}C_{\mathbb{A}}^{-1}\|\Lambda^{r}_{\#}\widetilde{\mathbf{u}}_{m}\|_{\dot{\mathbf{H}}_{\#\sigma}^{1}}^{2}=\frac{1}{4}C_{\mathbb{A}}^{-1}\|\widetilde{\mathbf{u}}_{m}\|^{2}_{\dot{\mathbf{H}}_{\#\sigma}^{r+1}}.

(4.20)

Let us now estimate the terms in the right hand side of (
4.19
).
First,

\displaystyle\langle\Lambda^{r-1}_{\#}\mathbf{f},\Lambda^{r+1}_{\#}\widetilde{\mathbf{u}}_{m}\rangle_{\mathbb{T}}\leq\|\Lambda^{r-1}_{\#}\mathbf{f}\|_{\dot{\mathbf{H}}_{\#}^{0}}\|\Lambda^{r+1}_{\#}\widetilde{\mathbf{u}}_{m}\|_{\dot{\mathbf{H}}_{\#}^{0}}\leq\|\mathbf{f}\|_{\dot{\mathbf{H}}_{\#}^{r-1}}\|\widetilde{\mathbf{u}}_{m}\|_{\dot{\mathbf{H}}_{\#}^{r+1}}.

(4.21)

Next, inequality (
1.20
) implies

\displaystyle\langle\nabla\Lambda^{r}_{\#}\mathbf{v}_{m},\nabla\Lambda^{r}_{\#}\widetilde{\mathbf{u}}_{m}\rangle_{\mathbb{T}}

\displaystyle\leq\|\nabla\Lambda^{r}_{\#}\mathbf{v}_{m}\|_{L_{2\#}^{n\times n}}\|\nabla\Lambda^{r}_{\#}\widetilde{\mathbf{u}}_{m}\|_{L_{2\#}^{n\times n}}

\displaystyle\leq\|\Lambda^{r}_{\#}\mathbf{v}_{m}\|_{\dot{\mathbf{H}}_{\#}^{1}}\|\Lambda^{r}_{\#}\widetilde{\mathbf{u}}_{m}\|_{\dot{\mathbf{H}}_{\#}^{1}}\leq\|\mathbf{v}_{m}\|_{\dot{\mathbf{H}}_{\#}^{r+1}}\|\widetilde{\mathbf{u}}_{m}\|_{\dot{\mathbf{H}}_{\#}^{r+1}}.

(4.22)

Further, since
\tilde{\sigma}+1>n/2
, we obtain by Theorem
6.1
(a), relation (
1.2
), and inequality (
1.20
),

\left|\left\langle E_{j\beta}({\bf v}_{m}),a_{ij}^{\alpha\beta}\Lambda^{r}_{\#}E_{i\alpha}(\Lambda^{r}_{\#}\widetilde{\mathbf{u}}_{m})\right\rangle_{{\mathbb{T}}}\right|\leq\|E_{j\beta}({\mathbf{v}}_{m})\|_{({H}_{\#}^{r})^{n\times n}}\|a_{ij}^{\alpha\beta}\Lambda^{r}_{\#}E_{i\alpha}(\Lambda^{r}_{\#}\widetilde{\mathbf{u}}_{m})\|_{({H}_{\#}^{-r})^{n\times n}}\\
\leq\|\mathbb{A}\|\|\widetilde{\mathbf{u}}_{m}\|_{\dot{\mathbf{H}}_{\#}^{r+1}},

(4.23)

where

\|\mathbb{A}\|:=\left\|\left\{a_{ij}^{\alpha\beta}\right\}_{\alpha,\beta,i,j=1}^{n}\right\|_{F}
.
Finally,

\left|\langle\Lambda^{r-1}_{\#}[({\mathbf{u}}_{m}\cdot\nabla){\mathbf{u}}_{m}],\Lambda^{r+1}_{\#}\widetilde{\mathbf{u}}_{m}\rangle_{{\mathbb{T}}}\right|\leq\|\Lambda^{r-1}_{\#}[({\mathbf{u}}_{m}\cdot\nabla){\mathbf{u}}_{m}]\|_{\dot{\mathbf{H}}_{\#}^{0}}\|\Lambda^{r+1}_{\#}\widetilde{\mathbf{u}}_{m}\|_{\dot{\mathbf{H}}_{\#}^{0}}\\
\leq\|({\mathbf{u}}_{m}\cdot\nabla){\mathbf{u}}_{m}\|_{\dot{\mathbf{H}}_{\#}^{r-1}}\|\widetilde{\mathbf{u}}_{m}\|_{\dot{\mathbf{H}}_{\#}^{r+1}}.

(4.24)

Implementing (
4.20
)–(
4.24
) in (
4.19
) and using Young’s inequality, we obtain

\frac{d}{dt}\left\|\widetilde{\mathbf{u}}_{m}\right\|_{\mathbf{H}^{r}_{\#}}^{2}+\frac{1}{2}C_{\mathbb{A}}^{-1}\|\widetilde{\mathbf{u}}_{m}\|_{\mathbf{H}_{\#}^{r+1}}^{2}\leq 2\Big(\|\mathbf{f}\|_{\mathbf{H}_{\#}^{r-1}}+\big[\|\mathbb{A}\|+1\big]\|\mathbf{v}_{m}\|_{\dot{\mathbf{H}}_{\#}^{r+1}}+\left\|({\mathbf{u}}_{m}\cdot\nabla){\mathbf{u}}_{m}\right\|_{\mathbf{H}_{\#}^{r-1}}\Big)\|\widetilde{\mathbf{u}}_{m}\|_{\mathbf{H}_{\#}^{r+1}}\\
\leq 4C_{\mathbb{A}}\Big(\|\mathbf{f}\|_{\mathbf{H}_{\#}^{r-1}}+\big[\|\mathbb{A}\|+1\big]\|\mathbf{v}_{m}\|_{\dot{\mathbf{H}}_{\#}^{r+1}}+\left\|({\mathbf{u}}_{m}\cdot\nabla){\mathbf{u}}_{m}\right\|_{\mathbf{H}_{\#}^{r-1}}\Big)^{2}+\frac{1}{4}C_{\mathbb{A}}^{-1}\|\widetilde{\mathbf{u}}_{m}\|_{\mathbf{H}_{\#}^{r+1}}^{2}.

Hence by the inequality
(\sum_{i=1}^{k}a_{i})^{2}\leq k\sum_{i=1}^{k}a_{i}^{2}
(following from the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality),

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}\left\|\widetilde{\mathbf{u}}_{m}\right\|_{\mathbf{H}^{r}_{\#}}^{2}+\frac{1}{4}C_{\mathbb{A}}^{-1}\|\widetilde{\mathbf{u}}_{m}\|_{\mathbf{H}_{\#}^{r+1}}^{2}\leq 16C_{\mathbb{A}}\Big(\|\mathbf{f}\|_{\mathbf{H}_{\#}^{r-1}}^{2}+\big[\|\mathbb{A}\|^{2}+1\big]\|\mathbf{v}_{m}\|_{\dot{\mathbf{H}}_{\#}^{r+1}}^{2}+\left\|({\mathbf{u}}_{m}\cdot\nabla){\mathbf{u}}_{m}\right\|_{\mathbf{H}_{\#}^{r-1}}^{2}\Big).

(4.25)

Note that by the similar reasoning, but without employing in (
4.9
)–(
4.10
) the function
\mathbf{v}
, we obtain that
\mathbf{u}_{m}
satisfies the differential inequality

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}\left\|\mathbf{u}_{m}\right\|_{\mathbf{H}^{r}_{\#}}^{2}+\frac{1}{4}C_{\mathbb{A}}^{-1}\|\mathbf{u}_{m}\|_{\mathbf{H}_{\#}^{r+1}}^{2}\leq 8C_{\mathbb{A}}\Big(\|\mathbf{f}\|_{\mathbf{H}_{\#}^{r-1}}^{2}+\left\|({\mathbf{u}}_{m}\cdot\nabla){\mathbf{u}}_{m}\right\|_{\mathbf{H}_{\#}^{r-1}}^{2}\Big).

(4.26)

Let us also estimate the last term in (
4.25
) and (
4.26
) for the case
n/2-1\leq r<n/2
.
By relation (
1.20
), the multiplication Theorem
6.1
(b), and the Sobolev interpolation inequality (
6.16
) we obtain,

\displaystyle\left\|({\mathbf{u}}_{m}\cdot\nabla){\mathbf{u}}_{m}\right\|_{\mathbf{H}_{\#}^{r-1}}^{2}=\left\|\nabla\cdot({\mathbf{u}}_{m}\otimes{\mathbf{u}}_{m})\right\|_{\mathbf{H}_{\#}^{r-1}}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\left\|{\mathbf{u}}_{m}\otimes{\mathbf{u}}_{m})\right\|_{({H}_{\#}^{r})^{n\times n}}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq C^{2}_{*rn}\|\mathbf{u}_{m}\|_{{\mathbf{H}}_{\#}^{r/2+n/4}}^{4}\leq C^{2}_{*rn}\|\mathbf{u}_{m}\|_{{\mathbf{H}}_{\#}^{r}}^{2}\|\mathbf{u}_{m}\|^{2}_{{\mathbf{H}}_{\#}^{n/2}},

(4.27)

where
C_{*rn}=C_{*}(r/2+n/4,r/2+n/4,n)
.

4.3
Serrin-type solution existence for constant anisotropic viscosity coefficients

Employing the results from Section
4.2
for
r=n/2-1
, we are now in the position to prove the existence of Serrin-type solutions.

THEOREM 4.1
.

Let
n\geq 2
, and
T>0
.
Let the coefficients
a_{ij}^{\alpha\beta}
be constant and the relaxed ellipticity condition (
1.4
) hold.
Let
{\mathbf{f}}\in L_{2}(0,T;\dot{\mathbf{H}}_{\#}^{n/2-2})
and
\mathbf{u}^{0}\in\dot{\mathbf{H}}_{\#\sigma}^{n/2-1}
.

(i) Then there exist constants
A_{1}\geq 0
,
A_{2}\geq 0
and
A_{3}>0
that are independent of
{\mathbf{f}}
and
\mathbf{u}^{0}
but may depend on
T
,
n
,
\|\mathbb{A}\|
and
C_{\mathbb{A}}
, such that
if
{\mathbf{f}}
,
\mathbf{u}^{0}
and
T_{*}\in(0,T]
satisfy the inequality

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T_{*}}\|\mathbf{f}(\cdot,t)\|_{\mathbf{H}_{\#}^{n/2-2}}^{2}dt+\left(A_{1}\|{\mathbf{u}}^{0}\|^{2}_{{\mathbf{H}}_{\#}^{n/2-1}}+A_{2}\right)\int_{0}^{T_{*}}\|(K{\mathbf{u}}^{0})(\cdot,t)\|_{\dot{\mathbf{H}}_{\#}^{n/2}}^{2}dt<A_{3},

(4.28)

where
K
is the operator defined in (
4.3
),
then there exists a solution
\mathbf{u}
of the anisotropic Navier-Stokes
initial value problem (
2.1
)–(
2.3
) in

L_{\infty}(0,T_{*};\dot{\mathbf{H}}_{\#\sigma}^{n/2-1})\cap L_{2}(0,T_{*};\dot{\mathbf{H}}_{\#\sigma}^{n/2})
, which is thus a Serrin-type solution.

(ii) In addition,

\mathbf{u}^{\prime}\in L_{2}(0,T_{*};\dot{\mathbf{H}}_{\#\sigma}^{n/2-2}),

\mathbf{u}\in\mathcal{C}^{0}([0,T_{*}];\dot{\mathbf{H}}_{\#\sigma}^{n/2-1})
,

\lim_{t\to 0}\|\mathbf{u}(\cdot,t)-{\mathbf{u}}^{0}\|_{\dot{\mathbf{H}}_{\#\sigma}^{n/2-1}}=0,

and
p\in L_{2}(0,T_{*};\dot{H}_{\#}^{n/2-1})
.

(iii) Moreover,
\mathbf{u}
satisfies the following energy equality for any
[t_{0},t]\subset[0,T_{*}]
,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\|{\mathbf{u}}(\cdot,t)\|_{\mathbf{L}_{2\#}}^{2}+\int_{t_{0}}^{t}a_{{\mathbb{T}}}({\mathbf{u}}(\cdot,\tau),{\mathbf{u}}(\cdot,\tau))d\tau=\frac{1}{2}\|{\mathbf{u}}(\cdot,t_{0})\|^{2}_{\mathbf{L}_{2\#}}+\int_{t_{0}}^{t}\langle\mathbf{f}(\cdot,\tau),{\mathbf{u}}(\cdot,\tau)\rangle_{{\mathbb{T}}}d\tau.

(4.29)

It particularly implies the standard energy equality,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\ {\mathbf{u}}(\cdot,t)\|_{\mathbf{L}_{2\#}}^{2}+\int_{0}^{t}a_{{\mathbb{T}}}({\mathbf{u}}(\cdot,\tau),{\mathbf{u}}(\cdot,\tau))d\tau=\frac{1}{2}\|{\mathbf{u}}^{0}\|^{2}_{\mathbf{L}_{2\#}}+\int_{0}^{t}\langle\mathbf{f}(\cdot,\tau),{\mathbf{u}}(\cdot,\tau)\rangle_{{\mathbb{T}}}\,d\tau\quad\forall\,t\in[0,T_{*}].

(4.30)

(iv) The solution
\mathbf{u}
is unique in the class of solutions from

L_{\infty}(0,T_{*};\dot{\mathbf{H}}_{\#\sigma}^{0})\cap L_{2}(0,T_{*};\dot{\mathbf{H}}_{\#\sigma}^{1})

satisfying the energy inequality (
2.19
) on the interval
[0,T_{*}]
.

Proof.

(i)
Let
r=n/2-1
.
Estimate (
4.27
) implies

\displaystyle\left\|({\mathbf{u}}_{m}\cdot\nabla){\mathbf{u}}_{m}\right\|_{\mathbf{H}_{\#}^{r-1}}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq C^{2}_{*rn}\|\mathbf{u}_{m}\|_{{\mathbf{H}}_{\#}^{r/2+n/4}}^{4}\leq 8C^{2}_{*rn}(\|\widetilde{\mathbf{u}}_{m}\|^{4}_{{\mathbf{H}}_{\#}^{r/2+n/4}}+\|{\mathbf{v}}_{m}\|^{4}_{{\mathbf{H}}_{\#}^{r/2+n/4}})

\displaystyle\leq 8C^{2}_{*rn}\|\widetilde{\mathbf{u}}_{m}\|^{2}_{{\mathbf{H}}_{\#}^{r}}\|\widetilde{\mathbf{u}}_{m}\|^{2}_{{\mathbf{H}}_{\#}^{n/2}}+8C^{2}_{*rn}\|{\mathbf{v}}_{m}\|^{2}_{{\mathbf{H}}_{\#}^{r}}\|{\mathbf{v}}_{m}\|^{2}_{{\mathbf{H}}_{\#}^{n/2}}

\displaystyle\leq 8C^{2}_{*rn}\|\widetilde{\mathbf{u}}_{m}\|^{2}_{{\mathbf{H}}_{\#}^{n/2-1}}\|\widetilde{\mathbf{u}}_{m}\|^{2}_{{\mathbf{H}}_{\#}^{n/2}}+8C^{2}_{*rn}\|{\mathbf{v}}_{m}\|^{2}_{{\mathbf{H}}_{\#}^{n/2-1}}\|{\mathbf{v}}_{m}\|^{2}_{{\mathbf{H}}_{\#}^{n/2}},

(4.31)

where
C^{2}_{*rn}:=C_{*n/2-1,n}=C_{*}(n/2-1/2,n/2-1/2,n)
.
Then by (
4.31
) we obtain from (
4.25
),

\frac{d}{dt}\left\|\widetilde{\mathbf{u}}_{m}\right\|_{\mathbf{H}^{n/2-1}_{\#}}^{2}+\frac{1}{4}C_{\mathbb{A}}^{-1}\|\widetilde{\mathbf{u}}_{m}\|_{\mathbf{H}_{\#}^{n/2}}^{2}\leq 128C^{2}_{*rn}C_{\mathbb{A}}\|\widetilde{\mathbf{u}}_{m}\|^{2}_{{\mathbf{H}}_{\#}^{n/2}}\|\widetilde{\mathbf{u}}_{m}\|^{2}_{\dot{\mathbf{H}}_{\#}^{n/2-1}}\\
+16C_{\mathbb{A}}\left(\|\mathbf{f}\|_{\mathbf{H}_{\#}^{n/2-2}}^{2}+8C^{2}_{*rn}\|{\mathbf{v}}_{m}\|^{2}_{{\mathbf{H}}_{\#}^{n/2-1}}\|{\mathbf{v}}_{m}\|^{2}_{{\mathbf{H}}_{\#}^{n/2}}+\big[\|\mathbb{A}\|^{2}+1\big]\|\mathbf{v}_{m}\|_{\dot{\mathbf{H}}_{\#}^{n/2}}^{2}\right).

(4.32)

Let us now apply to (
4.32
) Lemma
6.11
with

\displaystyle\eta=\left\|\widetilde{\mathbf{u}}_{m}\right\|_{\mathbf{H}^{n/2-1}_{\#}}^{2},\ \eta_{0}=0,\ y=\|\widetilde{\mathbf{u}}_{m}\|_{\mathbf{H}_{\#}^{n/2}}^{2},\ b=\frac{1}{4}C_{\mathbb{A}}^{-1},\ c=128C^{2}_{*rn}C_{\mathbb{A}},\
